Letter from the Mayor

Jody Carney

What an exciting time it is to be in Plain City!

Over the past year and a half, our community has come together

in thoughtful and intentional ways to shape a shared vision for our

future. This process has been rooted in meaningful conversations, and I

am truly grateful to everyone who took the time to participate. Every response, whether shared

in person or online, has played an important role in guiding this plan.

At our community workshops, I had the opportunity to meet many of you face to face and hear

directly what makes Plain City so special. Those conversations left a lasting impression on me.

Time and again, I heard how much you value our community character, our small-town pride,

and the sense of connection that defines this Village. Those priorities are not going away. In

fact, they have guided this plan from the very beginning.

We made a clear commitment to honor Plain City’s rich history while also embracing fresh

ideas, the realities of a growing Central Ohio region, and the unique opportunities ahead. There

is no doubt that growth brings complex challenges, but throughout this process, the Village has

remained mindful of all who call Plain City home.

As a farm girl growing up, I understand the importance of agricultural roots and the

responsibility we carry to preserve them. At the same time, I recognize the need to adapt

and plan for the future. Plain City represents a special balance of both. We are committed to

maintaining our agricultural heritage while investing in infrastructure, supporting thoughtful

growth, and making local decisions that benefit residents of all ages.

This Comprehensive Plan serves as a guide for that work. It reflects our shared values,

strengthens partnerships, and helps us make informed decisions about where and how we grow.

It identifies opportunities, sets priorities, and ensures that our actions align with the vision you

helped create.

This is also a living document. As our community evolves, so too will this plan, allowing us to

respond to new challenges and opportunities with care and purpose.

The best is yet to come. Together, we are building a future that respects our past, reflects who

we are, and prepares Plain City for generations to come.

With gratitude,

Mayor Jody Carney

Village of Plain City

INTRODUCTION 5

INTRODUCTION

Reflecting on Plain City’s past, outlining the

planning process, and framing the unique

moment shaping the Village’s next chapter.

Plain City is at a defining moment. Long known for its rural character, strong

community values, and small-town feel, the Village is now experiencing

steady growth shaped by new development, expanding infrastructure, and

regional change across Central Ohio. These shifts bring opportunity—but also

responsibility.

Plain City has planned before. The 2018 Comprehensive Plan established a

foundation for managing growth and protecting the qualities that make the

community unique. Since then, conditions have evolved. Population growth

has accelerated, infrastructure investments are underway, and the Village

is preparing for a transition to city status later this decade. Together, these

changes signal the need to look ahead once again.

The Picture Plain City Comprehensive Plan Update is a continuation of that

legacy of foresight. It asks important questions about how and where the

Village grows, how infrastructure and amenities keep pace, and how Plain City

can welcome new opportunities without losing its identity. Most importantly,

it reflects the voices of residents, business owners, and stakeholders who care

deeply about the future of this community.

Planning is how communities take ownership of change.  This

Comprehensive Plan Update provides a clear, community-driven framework

to guide growth, investment, and decision-making, ensuring that as Plain City

evolves, it remains rooted in the values that make it special.

6 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

INTRODUCTION 7

ABOUT THIS PLAN

In 2025, the Village of Plain City began the process of

updating its 2018 Comprehensive Plan to respond to

changing conditions and prepare for the next chapter of

growth. The Comprehensive Plan serves as the Village’s

long-range policy framework, guiding decisions related

to land use, housing, transportation, parks, economic

development, and public infrastructure.

This Plan Update builds on the foundation established

in 2018 while reflecting new realities of expanded water

and sewer service, increased development interest,

regional growth pressures, and community priorities that

have emerged through engagement. The Comprehensive

Plan translates the community’s vision into goals,

strategies, and recommendations that will help align

future investments and day-to-day decisions with a

shared vision.

As a policy guide, the Comprehensive Plan supports

Village Council, boards and commissions, Village staff,

and partners in making coordinated, transparent, and

informed choices that promote long-term quality of life.

What is a Comprehensive Plan?

A comprehensive plan serves as an official policy

document that guides a community and its partners in

shaping the future. It provides a long-term vision for

Plain City and a framework for decisions on land use,

housing, transportation, and more.

This Plan Update outlines the community’s goals,

strategies, and action recommendations across the plan

pillars: Pursue Smart Growth & Land Stewardship,

Strengthen Our Homes & Neighborhoods, Grow Jobs

& Local Business, Expand and Care for Our Parks,

Connect Our Community, Build on Momentum in

Uptown, Support Community Services & Partners, Lead

with Good Governance.

8 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

By setting a unifying vision, the Village can better

coordinate investments, policies, and development

patterns over time.

Why Update the Plan?

The 2018 Comprehensive Plan provided a strong starting

point that has been supported by subsequent planning

efforts, but Plain City continues to evolve. Key factors

shaping this Plan Update include:

• Expansion of water and wastewater infrastructure

• Regional growth and development pressure

• Preparation for city status

• Community interests in additional amenities, parks

and recreation, trails, and local businesses.

The Plain City Comprehensive Plan (2018) guided the

planning process by shaping earlier community priorities

and experiences, while also informing goal setting for

this Plan Update.

Guiding Pillars

The Comprehensive Plan serves not only as a road

map and foundation for coordinated action but it also

translates the community values into clear policies,

identifies opportunities, and sets priorities that will

shape the Village's long-term success. The foundation of

Plain City’s Comprehensive Plan is built on several key

elements:

1. Build on meaningful community engagement.

Continue the conversation started through past

planning efforts by engaging residents, businesses,

and stakeholders in shaping Plain City’s future.

2. Integrate and strengthen partnerships.  Coordinate

with neighboring jurisdictions, agencies, and

community organizations to support long-term

success and shared outcomes.

3. Ground decisions in data and local understanding.

Develop a comprehensive community profile that

reflects Plain City’s current conditions, systems, and

future needs to inform thoughtful decision-making.

4. Translate community values into action.

Establish clear goals, strategies, and action items

that reflect the community’s priorities and guide

implementation over time.

5. Focus growth through priority areas and

implementation strategies.  Identify key focus

areas and opportunities to convey the community’s

vision and direct future investment, growth, and

preservation efforts.

Ultimately, the Comprehensive Plan is a living document

designed to evolve over time as community needs shift

and new opportunities arise. It is a proactive tool to help

Plain City grow thoughtfully, equitably, and sustainably

into the future.

PREVIOUS PLANS

The Village has developed a handful

of related planning documents

that shaped the direction for the

Picture Plain City Comprehensive

Plan Update.  These previous plans

helped initiate goal setting and

ongoing efforts that continue to

shape the community's priorities and

experiences today.

• Thoroughfare Plan (2009): Laid

out a vision for managing traffic

flow, improving road networks, and

accommodating future growth by

identifying key road improvement

projects to enhance safety and

promote efficient travel.

• Plain City Comprehensive Plan

(2018): Outlined a shared vision

and set of strategies to manage

growth, coordinate infrastructure,

and support quality of life, serving

as the Village’s primary policy

decision-making framework.

• Uptown Master Plan (2023):

Established a plan for

development, public investment,

and design in the Uptown District

to strengthen its role as the

Village’s historic and economic

center.

• Plain City Economic Development

Strategic Plan (2023): Proposed

key initiatives to proactively shape

growth and strengthen the local

economy, including establishing

economic development tools,

supporting Uptown revitalization,

planning for new employment

areas, and coordinating with

regional partners.

INTRODUCTION 9

PLAN PROCESS

Community Understanding

The plan process began with a thorough assessment

of Plain City’s existing physical, demographic and

economic conditions and the community’s relative

position in Central Ohio. This included a community

tour and audit, Advisory Committee kick-off meeting,

Community Workshop, stakeholder interviews, and

existing conditions analysis. This baseline understanding

of Plain City resulted in a series of maps and analysis

findings, as well as a communications and engagement

plan. These assets set a course for a robust community

driven planning process tailored to the unique needs and

aspirations of Plain City.

Community Visioning

The visioning process invited residents and business

owners to think big about the future of Plain City,

helping the Planning Team understand what matters

most to the community. Input gathered through

Community Open Houses and Advisory Committee

meetings shaped the planning principles and community

priorities that guide this Plan Update. Building on that

foundation, Village Staff Workshops refined ideas from

the 2018 Comprehensive Plan, including placemaking,

land use, development, and transportation, into clear,

actionable recommendations.

Synthesis

Building on the community review and direction of

the planning process, the visioning, strategies and plan

concepts were synthesized into a complete document.

The Comprehensive Plan Update was then reviewed

by the community and the planning and zoning

commission before being adopted by Village Council.

INTRODUCTION 11

PLAN CONTEXT

The Village of Plain City is located in central Ohio at

the intersection of Union County and Madison County,

20 miles northwest of Columbus. The Village sits along

key regional corridors, including U.S. Route 33, U.S.

Route 42, and State Route 161, that connect it to nearby

communities and employment centers such as Dublin,

Marysville, and Columbus. These connections place Plain

City at the edge of one of the country's fastest-growing

metropolitan areas, where suburban growth transitions to

the agricultural landscapes that continue to characterize

the Village and its surroundings.

The Village is surrounded by a mix of rural townships,

growing suburban communities, and emerging

employment areas. Commercial and institutional growth

along SR-161 and Industrial Parkway has expanded

nearby economic activity, while farmland and open space

remain a defining feature of the surrounding landscape.

Plain City is also located within the watershed of Big

Darby Creek, one of Ohio’s most ecologically significant

waterways. Tributaries of the creek pass through the

community, placing environmental stewardship at the

center of long-term land use decisions.

Infrastructure systems and regional relationships will

also shape the Village’s future. The Mid-Ohio Water &

Sewer District is planning new water and wastewater

infrastructure serving Plain City and portions of

Madison County. These investments may enable new

development in areas surrounding the Village and could

generate future annexation requests as landowners

seek municipal services. Nearby communities are also

planning for growth, and infrastructure and land use

decisions in cities like Dublin and Marysville will

impact Plain City. Together, these overlapping systems,

transportation, utilities, environmental resources, and

neighboring jurisdictions place Plain City in a unique

position to guide growth, coordinate with regional

partners, and protect the natural and community assets

that define the Village.

Spanning a growing footprint in the northwest Columbus region, Plain

City is home to a mix of established neighborhoods, new residential

developments, and a small but evolving Uptown. The Village supports

a range of local businesses, light industrial activity, and access to nearby

employment centers, while maintaining its historic small-town character.

Parks, open space, and nearby rural landscapes contribute to a strong sense

of place and quality of life. .

Plain City blends its agricultural roots with steady growth, offering a

balance of community identity, expanding housing options, and regional

connectivity. As development pressures continue, this Comprehensive Plan

positions the Village to preserve its character, enhance key assets, and guide

thoughtful, well-managed growth into the future.

INTRODUCTION 13

History

Originally the home to Wyandotte and Mingo Native

American nations, the village plat for Plain City, then

named the Village of Westminster, was initiated in 1818

by town founder Isaac Bigelow. Originally a marshy

plain, the town was settled at a high point along the Big

Darby Creek. In the following decades, the community’s

name changed to Pleasant Valley and the construction

of the railroad supported the expansion of the frontier

community as a growing center of stockyards and

agriculture.

In 1886 four Amish families arrived in the area and

established a congregation. The area's Amish and

Mennonite population then grew to more than 300

families by some estimates and the population was well

established for nearly a century. Though much of the Old

Amish community left sometime after 1970, Amish and

Mennonite families and culture are still active parts of

community life today.

After the 1970s, the Village experienced new

development, mostly residential subdivision and some

highway-oriented development within the US 42

corridor. This suburban-type growth nearly doubled the

Village’s population between 1970 and the present. The

21st century brought an influx of new housing, business

growth, and public facilities, while Plain City remained

connected to its agricultural roots, preserving a strong

sense of community and small-town values that continue

to shape the Village.

Community Profile

Today, Plain City is home to approximately 4,916

residents within 4.4 square miles  of land area. The

Village's development pattern reflects a mix of

established neighborhoods, emerging residential growth,

agricultural land, and a compact historic Uptown,

creating a distinct small-town character within a rapidly

urbanizing region.

in its growth. Understanding who lives in the Village

today and how those demographics are changing

provides essential context for guiding future land use,

housing, transportation, and community investments.

gathering, and local identity continues to shape Plain

INTRODUCTION 15

Regional Context

Plain City’s future is unfolding within one of the fastest

growing and most dynamic regions in the Midwest,

anchored by Columbus and spanning eleven counties.

Once defined by its agricultural landscapes and small

town roots, the region has seen sustained population

growth, booming job markets, and new investment

in technology, healthcare, advanced manufacturing,

and logistics. Major infrastructure corridors like US

33, I-70, and SR 161 serve as major arteries carrying

people, commerce, and opportunity, connecting

communities at the edge of the metropolitan core and

evolving into powerful economic engines that bring

jobs, development interest, and new residents. At the

same time, this rapid regional growth is reshaping land

use, transportation patterns, and housing demand,

creating both opportunities and pressures for smaller

communities like Plain City. Positioned at a literal and

figurative crossroads, the Village serves as a unique

gateway between Central Ohio’s urban core and its rural

landscapes. As surrounding areas attract new businesses

and residents, Plain City is poised to leverage its small

town character, strong community identity, and access

to natural and cultural resources to thoughtfully guide

growth, balance development with preservation, and

strengthen its role in the broader regional story.

INTRODUCTION 19

COMMUNITY PROFILE

This Plan Update is built on community

insight that defines shared priorities and

guides the Plan’s recommendations.

Picture Plain City y kicked off in April 2025 with a clear focus: to reconnect

with where the Village has been since the 2018 Comprehensive Plan and

to understand how this Plan Update can help guide Plain City’s future.

Throughout this process, the central question remained the same, how can

the 2026 Comprehensive Plan Update best prepare the community for what's

next. This effort explored the right questions to ask the community and how

those responses could meaningfully shape the plan’s recommendations.

Community engagement began in June 2025 with the first Advisory

Committee meeting, followed by the first community workshop in August.

These early conversations focused on identifying key challenges and

opportunities related to housing, economic growth, infrastructure, city services,

and community identity.

The engagement process included both in-person and digital conversations

with residents, businesses, community organizations, and other stakeholders.

By actively listening to a wide range of voices, the planning team gathered

insights that helped shape the plan’s priorities and strategies.

Grounded in meaningful community input and established planning

principles, the Comprehensive Plan builds on Plain City’s values, vision,

and previous planning efforts. It serves as a policy guide for Council,

boards and commissions, and staff as they make decisions and invest in the

Village’s future. This chapter highlights the engagement process, community

feedback and previews the key findings, demonstrating how local voices

directly informed the Plan Update and set the stage for Plain City’s growth,

conservation, and evolution over the next decade and beyond.

Community Engagement Snapshot

INTRODUCTION 21

INTRODUCTION

The Comprehensive Plan Update was guided by an

internal and collaborative engagement process designed

to ensure that the voices of residents, businesses, and

community partners help shape the future of Plain City.

Throughout the process, the planning team worked

to understand both the opportunities emerging from

regional growth and the identity residents want to

preserve as the community evolves.

Engagement activities were designed to reach people in

multiple ways, combining in-person conversations with

digital tools that allowed the community to participate

in their own way. Workshops, stakeholder discussions,

advisory committee meetings, and online engagement

helped capture a broad range of perspectives.

This chapter provided an overview of how the

engagement process unfolded and what was learned

along the way. It began with a snapshot of participation

and a summary of the planning process, followed by

the key themes that emerged from conversations with

the community. Together, these insights helped shape

the priorities and recommendations that guide the

Comprehensive Plan and reflect Plain City’s shared

vision for the future.

INTRODUCTION 23

WHAT IS YOUR VISION FOR PLAIN CITY?

PROCESS OVERVIEW

Methodology and Approach

The Plan was supported by a structured engagement

process that included Village leadership, community

stakeholders, and the public. Each group played a

distinct role in shaping the plan's direction and refining

its recommendations.

A four-member Working Group, composed of staff from

the Village Administration and Development Services

Departments, served as the primary liaison between the

Village and the Planning Team. This group coordinated

project activities, assembled data and resources, and

provided initial review of deliverables to ensure the

process remained aligned with the Village’s priorities and

ongoing initiatives.

A 13-member Advisory Committee representing local

organizations, boards, businesses, and community groups

met three times throughout the process. Committee

members reviewed findings, discussed emerging ideas,

and helped refine plan goals and recommendations

based on their knowledge of community needs and local

conditions.

Additional perspectives were gathered through targeted

stakeholder discussions and broader public input.

In September 2025, nine stakeholder focus groups

brought together 39 participants to identify key issues,

opportunities, and priorities for the Village’s future.

These conversations offered targeted insight from

business leaders, developers, educators, public safety

officials, regional partners, and organizations involved in

recreation and tourism.

Community members also participated in two

community workshops held in August 2025 and January

2026, which together drew nearly 100 attendees.

24 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Participants shared ideas, identified opportunity sites,

and provided feedback on draft plan priorities and

recommendations. Supplementing these events, an

online engagement platform offered surveys, interactive

mapping tools, and virtual versions of workshop

activities, enabling more residents to share their thoughts

throughout the project.

Together, these engagement efforts helped ensure

that the Comprehensive Plan reflects local experience,

informed discussion, and a shared understanding of the

opportunities and challenges facing Plain City.

Stakeholder focus group conversations provided critical

insight from the community on a variety of topics.

INTRODUCTION 27

KEY THEMES

Throughout the engagement process, the community shared priorities, concerns, and ideas for the Village’s future.

Clear themes began to emerge, revealing what matters most to the community. The following page highlights these

ideas, which directly informed the plan’s focus and recommendations.

Rural landscapes and natural systems define Plain City’s identity, but continued growth puts these

assets at risk. Expansion toward environmentally sensitive areas, both within the Village and through

less coordinated development in surrounding townships, could gradually diminish farmland, tree cover,

and ecological systems such as Big Darby Creek. Conservation tools, environmental stewardship, and

coordinated land use planning can help protect these landscapes while guiding responsible growth.

Plain City’s parks and recreation system is essential to creating strong community connections and

shared spaces.  Continued investment in new recreation options, park upgrades, and family‑focused

amenities can enhance daily life, boost participation, and encourage healthier lifestyles. By improving

accessibility and expanding programs, the Village can support a more active and connected community.

Transportation infrastructure restricts movement between neighborhoods, parks, and regional

destinations. Expanding pedestrian infrastructure, bike facilities, and strengthening connections to

Uptown, neighborhoods, and regional trails such as the Heritage Trail can create a safer and more

connected network.

Traffic volumes and roadway safety concerns are increasing as development expands along key

corridors.  Higher speeds and congestion impact safety and traffic flow. Coordinated roadway design,

traffic calming strategies, and corridor planning can help maintain safe and efficient travel while

protecting community character.

Uptown serves as the cultural and economic heart of Plain City, yet significant opportunities for

growth and revitalization remain. Strategic redevelopment and carefully planned infill can help

strengthen the district as a vibrant, walkable destination, supporting local businesses and community

life while still preserving the area’s distinctive historic character.

Small-town character is one of Plain City’s greatest strengths, but new development could

compromise this identity. W Without intentional design standards, placemaking efforts, and smart

growth policies, new growth can alter the Village’s character. Planning that emphasizes architectural

compatibility, placemaking, and inviting public spaces can help ensure new development reflects the

Village's small-town feel.

INTRODUCTION 29

RECOMMENDATIONS

Presents a clear set of goals, strategies,

and actions to guide Plain City’s growth,

investment, and preservation efforts.

The recommendations in this chapter represent the culmination of months of

listening, learning, and analyzing what matters most to Plain City. They are

rooted first in the voices of residents, business owners, and stakeholders who

shared their perspectives during the initial round of community engagement.

Early community workshops and Advisory Committee meetings identified

key opportunities and challenges related to growth, housing, economic

development, parks, transportation, and public services. These ideas were then

tested and refined through a second community workshop, ensuring that the

strategies reflected the priorities and aspirations of the broader community.

In addition to community input, the recommendations were informed by

careful analysis of local data, including demographic trends, housing patterns,

land use, and regional growth pressures. This evidence helped shape actionable

strategies that are both realistic and forward-looking, providing the Village

with a clear roadmap for growth, conservation, and investment.

Taken together, the recommendations translate Plain City’s vision and values

into concrete actions. They provide a framework to guide land use, strengthen

neighborhoods, support local businesses, expand parks and trails, improve

connectivity, and enhance governance. Each recommendation is designed to

help the Village manage change thoughtfully, preserve its character, and build

a resilient, vibrant, and connected community for years to come.

INTRODUCTION 31

PLAN FRAMEWORK

Plain City’s Comprehensive Plan is more than a

collection of ideas, it is a blueprint for action that reflects

the community’s values, vision, and aspirations. The

recommendations presented in this chapter are organized

through a clear framework that connects Plain City’s

long-term vision with practical steps for implementation.

This structure helps translate the ideas and priorities

identified through the planning process into coordinated

actions that can guide decisions, investments, and

partnerships over time.

At the heart of the framework is a simple progression—

from vision to action. It begins with a shared vision for

the future of Plain City and is supported by a series of

plan pillars that represent the community’s major priority

areas. The goals, strategies, and recommendations within

each pillar work together to define what success looks

like and how the Village can achieve its vision.

To help readers navigate this plan, each section follows a

consistent structure:

• Vision: A broad statement of the community’s

values and aspirations—what Plain City strives to be

in the future.

• Plan Pillar: A major focus area that organizes the

plan’s recommendations and reflects the community’s

long-term priorities. Each pillar represents an

important dimension of Plain City’s future,

such as growth management, housing, economic

development, parks, or connectivity.

• Goals:  Clear, high-level outcomes the Village aims

to achieve under each pillar.

• Strategy:  A guiding approach that outlines how

the Village can move toward achieving a goal.

Strategies provide direction and help frame the types

of policies, partnerships, and initiatives needed to

support the goal.

32 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Residents come together to watch and participate in the

Fourth of July parade.

• Recommendation: Specific actions, programs,

policies, or projects that implement a strategy.

Recommendations provide the practical steps the

Village and its partners can take to advance the plan.

While the Village government will play a central

role in implementing many recommendations, this

plan recognizes that achieving its vision will require

collaboration across community organizations, local

businesses, institutions, and residents. By linking the

vision, goals, strategies, and recommendations in a clear,

cohesive framework, this plan provides a roadmap for

Plain City to thoughtfully shape its future, guiding

growth, protecting what is valued, and embracing new

opportunities over the next decade and beyond.

INTRODUCTION 33

PLAN PILLARS

The Pillars represent the central focus of the Comprehensive Plan. They articulate the key direction for

Plain City’s future, a response to current conditions, community aspirations, and the opportunities ahead.

These statements serve as the foundation for the plan’s goals, strategies, and recommendations.

Pursue Smart Growth & Land Stewardship

Guide growth in a way that balances development opportunities with preservation of Plain

City’s rural character, natural resources, and agricultural heritage. By strategically identifying

areas for new housing, commercial, and mixed-use development while protecting farmland,

environmentally sensitive areas, and key corridors, the Village can ensure that growth is both

sustainable and well-coordinated. This approach prioritizes proactive land use planning,

thoughtful annexation, and long-term stewardship to maintain the Village’s distinct identity

while supporting future residents and businesses.

Strengthen Our Homes & Neighborhoods

Plain City’s neighborhoods are the foundation of its character and quality of life. Offering

diverse and attainable housing options ensures that residents of all ages and life stages can find

a home in the Village. Efforts include maintaining and modernizing the existing housing stock,

addressing affordability and accessibility, and introducing new housing variety. Strengthening

neighborhoods reinforces community cohesion, encourages lifelong residency, and fosters a

welcoming environment for both new and long-term residents.

Grow Jobs & Local Business

Supporting local businesses, encouraging entrepreneurship, and attracting employment

opportunities are central to Plain City’s economic vitality. By leveraging the Village’s regional

connections, school system, and strategic location along major corridors, Plain City can nurture

small businesses, strengthen its commercial hubs, and align workforce development with

emerging regional opportunities. Economic growth should focus on generating year-round

activity, supporting locally owned businesses, and ensuring that new investment enhances both

the community’s character and its long-term fiscal sustainability.

Expand and Care for Our Parks

Parks, trails, and open spaces are essential to Plain City’s quality of life. By investing in new

parks, enhancing existing facilities, and expanding trails and greenways, the Village can provide

spaces for recreation, social connection, and environmental stewardship. Thoughtful planning

ensures that parks are accessible to all residents, linked to neighborhoods and Uptown, and

designed to meet evolving recreational needs. Caring for these spaces preserves the Village’s

natural and cultural assets while creating lasting community benefits.

Connect Our Community

A safe, accessible, and multimodal transportation network strengthens connectivity across

Plain City. Prioritizing pedestrian and bicycle infrastructure, enhancing roadway safety, and

integrating regional trail and transit connections ensures residents can move throughout the

Village efficiently and safely. These investments support everyday travel, recreation, and economic

activity, while also fostering social interaction and linking neighborhoods, parks, and Uptown.

RECOMMENDATIONS 35

LAND STEWARDSHIP

Establish a clear, long

term growth framework

Where and how Plain City grows will shape the Village’s

long-term character, fiscal health, and environmental

stewardship. Growth decisions impact the cost of

services, the strength of the tax base, and the preservation

of valuable natural and agricultural resources, including

the Big Darby Creek watershed, prime farmland, and

the Village’s rural buffer. Thoughtful, focused growth

allows Plain City to accommodate new homes, jobs,

and services in areas that can be efficiently served while

protecting the landscape and character that residents

value.

To achieve this, Plain City will guide development into

planned growth areas where infrastructure, utilities, and

transportation can support new activity. Annexation,

land use, and utility policies will be coordinated to ensure

that regional growth benefits the Village, strengthens

its economic base, prevents the Village from becoming

landlocked by outside development, and avoids

unchecked sprawl. This approach balances opportunity

with stewardship, encouraging development in locations

that can accommodate it while maintaining open spaces,

natural features, and agricultural lands that define the

community’s identity.

By concentrating growth strategically, Plain City can

support housing and employment expansion without

sacrificing the qualities that make the Village unique.

The recommendations in this section provide tools and

guidance to ensure development is proactive, aligned

with community priorities, and responsive to the

pressures of a rapidly growing region. Through smart

growth and land stewardship, Plain City can continue to

welcome new residents and businesses while protecting

the natural and rural resources that contribute to the

Village’s long-term sustainability and quality of life.

PURSUE SMART GROWTH &

Conditions & Trends

882

Acres Annexed

Growth is occurring rapidly on Plain City’s edges.

Since 2022, the Village has annexed 882 acres of

land, reflecting continued demand for residential and

commercial development. These expansions are largely

occurring in areas with existing utility and transportation

access.

The breakdown of land uses in the Village reflect a

broader transition underway.  Historically, the Village

has been surrounded by farmland but had very little

land dedicated to agricultural use within its boundaries.

Today, 36% of the Village by land area is made up of

former agricultural land slated for development. Much

of this undeveloped land is located on recently annexed

parcels at the periphery where future development is

anticipated, reflecting the Village's long-term growth

strategy.

Planned Unit Developments (PUDs) are shaping the

Village’s neighborhoods. 270 acres are zoned for PUDs,

enabling large-acre, master-planned subdivisions that

balance housing growth with infrastructure planning and

community amenities.

please keep the

small town, rural

feel that defines

Plain City."

Plain City and the surrounding area is a prime market

for residential development. Residential land use is

the second largest use by total land area in Plain City,

making up 28% of land. Availability of large land

area surrounding the Village, proximity to regional

employment centers, and potential future wastewater

availability along key arterials all contribute to the

growing development pressure inside and surrounding

Plain City.

Environmental stewardship and proximity to the

Big Darby Creek inform development in Plain City.

Management (208) Plan, specifically Appendix 3-3,

extends Darby-related permit requirements to all

counties within the Big Darby Creek watershed.

Development Principles

A strong project in Plain City will…

• Fit the fabric.  Reinforce the contextual position of

the area with respect to neighborhood character;

compatible scale, frontage, and materials.

• Integrate logically. Pair complementary uses

vertically or horizontally to keep areas active

throughout the day. Plan sites thoughtfully. Organize

buildings, access, service, and parking to prioritize

people first.

• Connect clearly. Extend streets, sidewalks, and

paths to close gaps and link to regional trails and

parks. Integrate usable, connected open spaces with

amenities that encourage activity and community

gathering.

• Protect the Big Darby Creek. Utilize stormwater

and riparian best practices; avoid floodways and

respect sensitive areas. Treat waterways as a valuable

community asset.

• Accommodate for walking & biking.  Include

safe crossings, traffic calming, bike parking, shade,

lighting, and wayfinding.

• Diversify housing. Deliver a range of types to match

evolving, local needs.

• Strengthen Uptown. Reuse historic buildings,

fill gaps, and add upper-story housing; coordinate

hours/events with neighbors.

• Be infrastructure-ready. Before new development

happens, make sure the necessary infrastructure

(roads, water, sewer, utilities, stormwater, etc.) is in

place or planned.

• Show economic value. A Add taxable value, local

revenue generation, and job space without over

extending services.

• Celebrate and reinforce identity. Incorporate Plain

City’s history, art, and branding in new architecture

and public realm enhancements.

1.3 A Annexation & Utility Service Policy (Playbook)

Establish a playbook for annexation and utility extension

decisions to ensure that growth aligns with Village

priorities and service capacity.

Plain City should adopt a clear Annexation and Utility

Service Playbook to guide how future annexation

requests are evaluated. Under the Village’s new

service context, annexation can no longer be treated

primarily as an extension of Village-owned water and

sewer infrastructure. Instead, it should function as a

policy choice that allows the Village to be selective

about growth, particularly in the context of continued

development pressure at the periphery. While the Village

cannot halt this growth, annexation provides a critical

tool to help shape and manage it, reducing the risk of

incompatible patterns just beyond its boundaries. In

this way, annexation enables the Village to ask whether

a site, project, or land resource will create a strong

community addition in return for becoming part of Plain

City and being served through emerging coordinated

regional utility systems that support long-term growth.

Without annexation, development may still occur,

but without the benefit of Village design standards,

environmental protections, infrastructure coordination,

or long‑term fiscal alignment. This recommendation

builds on the Village’s existing annexation incentive

work while updating it for a different growth and utility

environment.

At a minimum, the Playbook should require that

annexation decisions align with the Growth and

Conservation Framework (Recommendation 1.1) and

produce clear public value. That value may take different

forms depending on the site: new housing types that

broaden the Village’s supply, employment uses that

strengthen long-term income tax performance, land uses

that reinforce quality of place, environmental protections

that preserve sensitive landscapes, or connections that

improve access to parks, trails, schools, and Uptown.

Aligning the Playbook to the Growth and Conservation

Framework allows decision makers to evaluate whether

growth meaningfully advances the Village’s goals for

identity, connectivity, economic resilience, and long

term fiscal health. Plain City's other existing plans

emphasize coordinated regional growth, stronger

community connections, environmental stewardship, and

strategic infrastructure decisions. By establishing clear

44 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Existing sewer services in Plain City extend as far north

as Pastime Park, servicing the adjacent neighborhoods.

Annexations and development proposals will require

careful evaluation of the current utility network to

determine where expansion is necessary and feasible.

expectations for when annexation is appropriate (and

when it is not), the Playbook helps ensure growth occurs

in a coordinated way. An Annexation and Utility Service

Playbook will help the Village structure its decision

making to advance progress towards these goals.

The Playbook will reinforce that annexation decisions

should clearly advance adopted Village priorities, align

with the Village's long-term land use vision, and be

supported by the Village's ability to efficiently provide

services and infrastructure. Priority will be given to

annexations that strengthen fiscal sustainability, enhance

connectivity, and support smart growth patterns.

While the Village will maintain communication with

neighboring jurisdictions where appropriate, annexation

decisions will ultimately be guided by the Village's

responsibility to protect its long-term growth, economic

vitality, service capacity, and financial stability within and

immediately surrounding its boundaries.

1.4 Big Darby Creek & Farmland Stewardship Toolkit

Provide tools, strategies, and policies to protect sensitive

natural areas and preserve working farmland.

Plain City should establish a Big Darby Creek and

Farmland Stewardship Toolkit to protect the natural

systems and working lands that remain central to the

community’s identity and long-term growth strategy.

This recommendation responds to two realities at once:

first, that the Village has an important responsibility

to help safeguard the Big Darby Creek and related

environmental resources; and second, that much of the

land shaping Plain City’s future will remain in private

ownership, requiring a mix of policy tools, incentives,

partnerships, and voluntary conservation approaches

rather than regulation alone.

This action is especially important considering Ohio

EPA’s recent update to the State Water Quality

Management (208) Plan for the Big Darby Creek

watershed. In 2024, Ohio EPA expanded the watershed’s

special protection measures beyond Franklin County

to nearly the full Big Darby Creek watershed, with

new emphasis on protecting stream setbacks, wetlands,

forested areas, and highly erodible slopes through

Appendix 3-3 of the 208 Plan. The Big Darby Creek

is widely recognized as one of Ohio’s most ecologically

significant watersheds, and protecting its water quality

requires approaches that go beyond conventional

development and stormwater practices. For Plain City,

that creates both a responsibility and an opportunity:

to align local planning, annexation, land use, and

development decisions with the heightened importance

of this regional ecological resource.

At the same time, the Toolkit should recognize that

farmland stewardship is more complex than simple

preservation. The Village cannot control all private land

decisions, but it can clearly signal that one of its desired

long-term outcomes is the maintenance of a meaningful

agricultural and open space buffer to protect Plain City

from rising development pressure from surrounding

townships. This agricultural buffer both supports

environmental stewardship capabilities and reinforces

Plain City's rural identity, increasing the Village's

legibility as a distinct place rather than a continuation of

regional sprawl.

The Toolkit should therefore combine protective and

enabling strategies: stronger local standards around

Big Darby Creek, Source: Anthony Sasson

TOOLS IN ACTION: PROTECTING

FARMLAND & NATURAL SYSTEMS

Communities use a mix of tools and partnerships

to protect farmland and sensitive natural areas

while accommodating growth. Agricultural

easements permanently limit development,

helping keep land in production while providing

financial support to landowners. Agricultural

Districts offer additional protections, including

defense against nuisance complaints and added

scrutiny of eminent domain.

These efforts are often supported by partners such

as the Madison Soil and Water Conservation

District, the Ohio Department of Agriculture’s

LAEPP, and the Ohio Department of Natural

Resources Scenic Rivers Program. Together,

these tools illustrate how policy, funding, and

partnerships can protect working lands and

waterways like the Big Darby Creek.

pollutants, stressors, and impervious cover. As this work

approach, allowing Plain City to continue evolving while

protecting the waterways, landscapes, and working lands

that define its edge and its value.

Define the intended long-term use of land across the

As part of this Comprehensive Plan Update, the Village

has updated its Future Land Use Map to reflect an

updated long-term vision for how land should be used,

conserved, and developed across the community. The

map (on page 39) establishes the Plain City’s intended

areas of growth and preservation, translating broad goals

for neighborhoods, economic development, open space,

infrastructure, and community character into a place

based framework.

The Future Land Use Map is informed by past and

future planning, helping the Village identify areas for

conservation, farmland retention, and environmentally Converse Rd

sensitive development. It provides Village leadership,

staff, residents, property owners, and the development

community with a shared understanding of how different

areas of Plain City are expected to function over US 42

time, supporting consistent and coordinated decisions SR 161

direction into parcel-level regulations, permitted uses, Main St

Gateway Corridor

A mixed-use corridor located along key entries into

the Village, characterized by predominantly non

residential uses such as retail, grocery, and dining, with

opportunities for residential, office, medical, and flex

industrial uses as part of integrated developments.

These corridors are designed to balance economic

activity with preservation of the Village’s rural character,

requiring significant building setbacks to maintain

viewsheds and accommodate shared-use paths, landscape

buffers, naturalized open space, and future roadway

improvements. Development is organized to minimize

direct access onto SR 161 and US 42, instead utilizing

shared access points and an internal street network

to support safe and efficient circulation. Buildings are

oriented toward public frontages or internal streets,

with parking located to the side or rear of principal

buildings. Continuous pedestrian and trail connections,

coordinated site design, and consistent landscaping

and screening standards contribute to a cohesive

corridor appearance while reducing visual and traffic

impacts along these primary gateways. Future planned

developments are encouraged to incorporate low

impact development and green infrastructure practices

to minimize any environmental impacts of impervious

surfaces and stormwater runoff.

Conservation/Protected

This designation includes environmentally sensitive areas

that are protected by state and federal regulations, in

addition to areas that should be considered for additional

protection on a case-by-case basis, to the greatest extent

feasible. This designation includes areas within the

Ohio EPA Max Stream Corridor Setback, wetlands,

highly erodible soils due to steep slopes, wooded areas

of greater than three acres in size, prominent tree rows,

and other areas that are important for protection to

allow for the creation of linkages among conservation

areas. Additional sensitive areas not currently mapped or

protected by regulation, which may be identified through

engineering surveys, should also be considered for

protection, mitigation, or restoration.

58 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

The Big Darby Creek is one of Plain City's most

important natural assets. With over 100 fish species,

45 freshwater mollusk species, and a variety

of riparian and prairie vegetation, the creek is

considered one of the most important waterways

in Central Ohio. As a designated State and National

Scenic River, the waterway requires specific

protections to preserve the remarkable biodiversity

of the creek and its surroundings.

161 JOINT PLANNING AREA

The SR 161 corridor is one of Plain City’s most

strategically important planning areas, serving as

both a primary gateway into the Village and a critical

connection to the region’s major employment centers

to the east. That role is becoming more significant as

the corridor shifts from a largely transitional landscape

to one shaped by active infrastructure expansion, major

land transactions, and pending development pressure.

Mid-Ohio Water & Sewer District has multiple

projects advancing along the corridor, including the

SR 161 sanitary sewer extension to the railroad, the

SR 161 water transmission line and water tower, and

related regional wastewater improvements, all of which

increase long-term development readiness. Meanwhile,

a recent 250-acre sale along Route 161 at approximately

$325,000 per acre underscores the intensity of market

interest, while Dublin’s publicly stated support for a

potential passenger rail stop near SR 161 and Houchard

Road points to even broader regional ambitions in the

corridor. In that context, the land between Kileville and

Plain City’s eastern gateway will be especially important

in defining success for the joint planning area, not

Currier Rd

PLAIN CITY

Cemetery Pike

Iams Rd

D A R B Y

TOWNSHIP

60 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

only in terms of land use, but in how well the corridor

balances access, infrastructure, development quality, and

the Village’s long-term identity.

The joint planning area is defined as much by its

jurisdictional complexity as by its development potential.

The corridor sits at the meeting point of multiple

communities and governing interests: Plain City at the

west, Dublin to the east, Darby Township in Madison

County occupying much of the land between and along

the corridor, and Jerome Township in Union County

immediately to the north. That quilt of jurisdictions is

overlaid by major infrastructure systems that will heavily

influence what happens next, including significant

planned water and sanitary sewer extensions by Mid

Ohio Water & Sewer District along SR 161 and US

42, as well as a major north-south electric transmission

corridor just east of Kileville. Mid-Ohio Water &

Sewer District’s published capital program includes

the SR 161 sanitary sewer extension to the railroad,

the SR 161 water transmission line and water tower,

and the US 42 force main connection to the regional

MADISON COUNTY

SR 161

FRANKLIN COUNTY

wastewater reclamation facility, with key construction

and completion milestones scheduled between 2026 and

2028. Together, these conditions mean the corridor is

not simply a development edge for one community, but

a shared and highly strategic geography where land use,

utilities, access, and long-term identity will need to be

negotiated across boundaries.

Defining Success Along the Joint Corridor

Long-term success in the SR 161 Joint Planning

Area will not be measured simply by how much

development occurs, but by whether the corridor

evolves in a coordinated way that protects Plain City’s

gateway character, matches infrastructure realities,

improves mobility, and positions the Village to respond

strategically to major regional change.

1. A rational land use framework. Success along the

corridor will depend on a clear land use framework

that matches development intensity to the realities

of infrastructure, access, and long-term community

goals. Growth should be directed in a way that does

not overwhelm existing or planned utility systems,

roadway capacity, or the Village’s ability to serve and

shape new development over time.

2. A protected rural gateway into Plain City. The

corridor should continue to feel like an entry into

Plain City rather than an uninterrupted strip of

frontage development. That means preserving

generous setbacks, protecting key rural viewsheds,

and building in enough open space along the

roadway to maintain a long-term sense of arrival,

openness, and transition into the Village.

3. A more disciplined access pattern. Success will

require strong access management and a more

coordinated transportation framework along the

corridor. New curb cuts should be minimized,

driveways and access points should be consolidated

where possible, and future development should be

organized around connected internal street networks

and shared intersections rather than a series of

disconnected frontage decisions.

4. Shared outcomes where impacts are shared.

Because the corridor spans multiple jurisdictions

and because the consequences of growth will not

stop at jurisdictional lines, the area calls for shared

win models that align economic benefit with shared

infrastructure and service demands. For locations

especially susceptible to change, tools such as

cooperative revenue-sharing or joint development

arrangements should be explored so that the costs

and benefits of development are addressed more

fairly and strategically.

5. Readiness for catalytic projects. Th The corridor should

be planned with enough flexibility and foresight to

respond to larger catalytic opportunities, especially

around Kileville and future passenger rail or station

area investment. Success means not just reacting if

such a project emerges, but understanding now how

it could reshape access, land values, development

pressure, and long-term opportunity for Plain City.

6. A multimodal connection eastward. Over the long

term, SR 161 should evolve into a corridor that

better connects Plain City to the east not only by

car, but by bike and on foot. A shared use path along

an upgraded 161 corridor would help link Uptown

and surrounding neighborhoods to the broader

regional network and create a meaningful active

transportation connection toward Dublin.

7. A common corridor character. As the corridor

urbanizes, it should do so with a consistent and

intentional public realm. Planted medians, street

trees, coordinated landscaping, and a common

aesthetic language can help ensure that corridor

improvements feel ordered and memorable rather

than piecemeal, while also reinforcing the importance

of SR 161 as a major gateway environment.

8. New north-south connections over time. As

development occurs, opportunities to improve north

south connectivity should be preserved and advanced,

including potential bypass or relief connections, such

as a SR 161-US 42 connector east of Plain City, that

reduce dependency on routing all movement through

Uptown. These connections will be important for

long-term circulation, truck movement, emergency

access, and the Village’s ability to balance growth

with the character of its historic core.

NEIGHBORHOODS

A mix of high-quality,

context sensitive housing

options

Strengthening Plain City’s homes and neighborhoods

is essential to maintaining the Village’s character while

supporting its continued growth. As Plain City evolves,

its neighborhoods will play a defining role in shaping the

community’s identity, livability, and long-term stability.

To become a community of choice while preserving

its small-town heritage and connection to the Big

Darby Creek landscape, the Village will welcome new

households through a wider mix of housing options in

walkable, connected neighborhoods, focusing growth in

areas where infrastructure and services can support it.

Plain City’s housing market reflects both opportunity

and pressure. Strong demand driven by quality schools,

regional employment access, and available land has

led to rapid residential growth, with nearly 900 new

housing units permitted in recent years. However,

most of this growth has been in single-family housing,

limiting options for young professionals, aging residents,

and others seeking more attainable or flexible housing

choices. Expanding the range of housing types,

including townhomes, duplexes, and small multi-family

developments, will help meet evolving needs while

supporting a more inclusive and resilient community.

At the same time, Plain City’s existing neighborhoods

are a key asset that must be preserved and enhanced. The

Village’s housing stock reflects a blend of historic homes

and newer construction, contributing to its unique

character. Continued investment in neighborhood quality

64 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

One of the single-family home designs available in the

Darby Station development by M/I Homes.

through maintenance, infrastructure improvements, and

access to parks and community amenities will ensure

these areas remain attractive, functional, and connected.

Walkability, safe streets, and proximity to destinations

will be critical in reinforcing neighborhood vitality and

supporting everyday life.

Strong, well-connected neighborhoods are how

Plain City converts growth into lasting community,

opportunity, and a stable tax base. By thoughtfully

planning for housing diversity, investing in

neighborhood infrastructure, and aligning development

with service capacity, the Village can ensure that growth

enhances rather than detracts from quality of life. The

recommendations in this section provide a framework

for guiding housing and neighborhood investment in a

way that supports long-term sustainability, strengthens

community connections, and preserves the character that

residents value most.

STRENGTHEN OUR HOMES &

Conditions & Trends

895

New Housing Units

since 2021

Home values in Plain City exceed average housing

costs in nearby counties, particularly Madison County.

In 2023, the median home value reached $325,400—

$19,000 higher than Union County and $126,000 higher

than Madison County.

Like much of the region, housing costs in Plain City

have risen significantly since 2000.  Median home

values have increased by 161%, or over $200,000, over

the past 25 years. Meanwhile, rental costs have risen

sharply, almost doubling during this period.

Plain City’s housing stock is expanding rapidly. Since

2021, the Village has approved 783 residential permits

for 895 new units. As a result, 34.3% of the Village’s

housing stock has been built or approved since 2020.

This far exceeds the share of housing built after 2020

in neighboring communities, where post-2020 housing

accounts for just 0.7% in Dublin, 1.7% in Hilliard, and

3.4% in Marysville.

Single-family detached homes dominate the real estate

landscape in Plain City, and recent construction trends

reinforce this pattern. Th These homes currently make

up 71.7% of all units, and 97.1% of residential permits

issued between 2021 and 2025 were for single-family

development. Once completed, single-family detached

homes are expected to comprise 80% of the Village’s

housing stock.

Multi-family development is limited and highly

concentrated. Most multi-family permits have been

issued for The Residences at Maren Reserve, a single

story townhome community with 132 units across 21

buildings completed, and 226 additional units across 35

buildings planned for construction. More broadly, the

shortage of “missing middle” housing—such as duplexes,

townhomes, and small apartment buildings—limits

options for residents priced out of the single-family

market.

The majority of homes are owner-occupied, accounting

for approximately three-quarters of all units. A Among

homeowners, 73.8% carry a mortgage. Housing costs are

relatively manageable for many homeowners, with more

than half of mortgage holders spending less than 20% of

their monthly income on housing.

HOUSING FOR ALL STUDY

Sun Prairie, WI

In 2022, the City of Sun Prairie adopted the

Housing For All study, which analyzed housing

conditions, identified unmet needs, and outlined

strategies to address housing challenges. The

plan set goals to expand housing supply, increase

affordable ownership and rental options, and

establish long-term coordination on housing

issues. Key recommendations included creating

dedicated housing staff and a housing committee,

updating the zoning code to permit more

flexibility and diversity in the housing stock,

offering financial assistance programs, and

establishing a community land trust.

Since adoption, the City hired dedicated housing

staff, created an Affordable Housing Fund Policy,

approved zoning updates and tax-increment

financing for new development, launched a

Tenant Resource Center, and built a centralized

housing website. The City has also approved

hundreds of new housing units each year since

adoption, advancing the plan’s goals.

66 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

2.1 Plain City Housing Strategy

Identify current and future housing needs and develop

goals that address housing for diverse income levels and

household types.

Plain City should prepare a comprehensive housing

strategy to better understand existing conditions and

anticipate future needs. As the Village grows, shifts in

demographics, housing costs, and household composition

may indicate a need for a broader range of housing

options than those currently available.

The strategy should evaluate housing supply, affordability,

and gaps in the current market, including opportunities

for first-time homebuyers, aging residents, and workforce

households. This effort should also identify appropriate

locations for different housing types based on access to

infrastructure, services, and amenities.

In addition to market analysis, the strategy should

outline implementation actions such as zoning updates,

development incentives, and partnerships that support

desired housing outcomes. By establishing a clear

housing strategy, Plain City can better align policy,

investment, and development decisions with long-term

community goals.

2.2 Sweeney Run & West Avenue Focus Area

Explore land use, transportation, and infrastructure

strategies for the Sweeney Run and West Avenue area

to support well-planned residential growth and a mix of

housing types.

The Sweeney Run and West Avenue area represents one

of Plain City’s most significant opportunities to shape

how the Village grows over the next decade. Located at

the edge of Uptown and directly connected to a future

trail and greenway spine, this area has the potential

to become a model for how new neighborhoods can

Sweeney Run is the location of the former

support walkability, expand housing choice, and reinforce Plain City Airport. The Airport was established

community character. in 1946 and operated for roughly 40 years.

To guide this growth, the Village should advance coordinated concept plans for both Sweeney Run and

West Avenue that establish a clear framework for land use, mobility, open space, and development patterns.

These concept plans move beyond existing subdivision approaches in Plain City by organizing development

around connected streets, integrated green space, and a mix of housing types that respond to evolving

market demands.

At Sweeney Run, the visionary diagram envisions a neighborhood structured around a continuous greenway

corridor, where trails, open space, and natural features become the organizing elements of the community.

Housing frontage should be built to face these spaces, encouraging an active and visible public realm, while

strengthening connections to the broader trail network and Uptown. This approach not only enhances

quality of life for residents but also creates a destination that draws people from across the Village.

Along West Avenue, the concept introduces a different but complementary housing pattern, including

townhomes and compact residential formats organized around shared courtyards and internal open spaces.

These configurations provide additional housing diversity while maintaining a strong sense of community

and walkability. Smaller lot sizes and attached housing types can help expand access to homeownership and

offer alternatives for residents seeking lower-maintenance living options.

The proximity of both areas to Uptown is a defining advantage. Future residents will be within walking or

biking distance of shops, dining, parks, and community events, supporting an active lifestyle and reinforcing

Uptown as the center of daily life. At the same time, these neighborhoods can help generate new foot traffic

and economic activity that benefits local businesses and strengthens the Village’s core. Infill development

is often accompanied by future thoroughfare planning and investments in safer streets and pedestrian

infrastructure. This is especially important as vehicular traffic continues to increase through Uptown.

Neighborhood-oriented infill development often reinforces pedestrian-focused streets, and over time

influences vehicular behavior, prompting investment into upgraded travel lanes and intersections.

Through concept planning, the Village can align infrastructure investment, ensure connectivity, and establish

development patterns that prioritize people, open space, and long-term value. This includes coordinating

utilities, street networks, and trail connections early in the process to avoid fragmented or inefficient

development.

As Plain City continues to grow, the Sweeney Run and West Avenue Focus Areas can serve as a model for

future neighborhoods, demonstrating how thoughtful planning, diverse housing, and strong connections to

open space and Uptown can create places that are livable and lasting.

STRENGTHEN OUR HOMES & NEIGHBORHOODS | RECOMMENDATIONS 69

SWEENEY RUN FOCUS AREA

29.5 acres

Sweeney Run Site, Source: Google Earth

The Sweeney Run Focus Area represents one of Plain

City’s most compelling redevelopment opportunities: an

in-town, undeveloped legacy site with deep local roots

and unusual proximity to the heart of the Village.

Once home to the former Plain City Airport, the area

traces its history back to 1946, when the airfield was

established by Bob Chapman, a member of a family long

associated with local enterprise and civic life. For several

decades, the site functioned as a community airfield,

with a simple landing strip, hangar, and supporting

businesses, before closing sometime in the mid-1980s.

While the planes are long gone, the story of the site

remains an important part of its identity. Today, the

roughly 30-acre area lies immediately east of US 42

and within a five-minute walk of Main Street and

Uptown, making it a central and highly connected piece

of Plain City’s future. It is bordered by Sweeney Run,

a tributary of Big Darby Creek that gives the area both

its name and its environmental character; by established

neighborhoods to the south and east; by businesses such

as Der Dutchman to the west; and by light industrial

properties north of the creek behind Main Street. This

setting places the focus area at the intersection of several

different development patterns and community assets.

It is a site where Plain City’s history, natural systems,

surrounding neighborhoods, and economic energy all

come into close contact, creating the opportunity for

70 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

a future district that feels authentically of the Village

and stitches together key parts of the community. The

Village’s planning work already identifies Sweeney

Run as a distinct character area and commercial center,

reinforcing its role as a meaningful place for thoughtful

reinvestment.

The Sweeney Run Focus Area is one of the few places

in Plain City where the Village can shape an entire new

district close to Uptown, rather than simply react to

incremental change. With its in-town location, proximity

to established neighborhoods, frontage along US 42,

and relationship to the Sweeney Run corridor, the area

is positioned to do important work for the community:

improve connections, expand economic opportunity,

strengthen ecological function, and create a memorable,

historically-significant neighborhood environment. That

is why the Village must be prepared with a clear vision

before development pressure defines the site on its own.

In Sweeney Run, success should be measured not just by

the amount of new investment attracted, but by how well

that investment connects people to Uptown, improves

the public realm, responds to the stream corridor, and

contributes to the long-term character and function of

Plain City.

Defining Success at Sweeney Run

1. Stronger neighborhood connections to and

through the site. Sweeney Run can help connect

Plain City’s fast-growing western and southern

neighborhoods back to Uptown through a more

complete network of sidewalks, trails, and local

streets. Rather than functioning as an isolated

project, the area can become an important link

in the Village’s broader walkability and mobility

framework.

2. Housing choice close to Uptown. Th The Sweeney

Run Focus Area can expand Plain City’s housing

mix with new homes that support a wider range

of life stages, from younger households and first

time buyers to empty nesters and older residents

seeking to stay close to community life. Its proximity

to Uptown creates the oppportunity to pair new

housing with walkable access to shops, restaurants,

services, events, and open space.

3. A linear park and restored stream corridor.

The Sweeney Run corridor can become one of

the defining public assets of the district. With

ecological restoration, native landscaping, trails, and

usable open space, the stream edge can shift from

a constraint or leftover condition into a central

amenity for both the neighborhood and the Village.

4. A new north-south local connection. Th The site

presents an opportunity to introduce a new local

north-south connection that offers an alternative

to US 42 for shorter trips. This would improve

circulation, better distribute traffic, and help tie

together neighborhoods, businesses, and Uptown

through a finer-grained street network.

5. New business opportunity along US 42.  Frontage

along US 42 creates room for new neighborhood

serving and visitor-oriented uses, such as lodging,

restaurants, or small-scale retail and service

businesses. This edge of the site can function as both

an economic opportunity and a gateway into the

district, helping meet local needs while creating a

stronger arrival experience.

6. A memorable neighborhood character.  Sweeney

Run can model a different kind of growth for Plain

City through tree-lined streets, strong landscaping,

usable open space, and front-porch-oriented homes.

The site also offers an opportunity to carry forward

the legacy of the former Plain City Airport through

naming, design details, interpretive elements, or

public spaces that reflect its history. In that way,

the neighborhood can feel not only walkable and

welcoming, but uniquely tied to the story of the

Village.

2.3 Missing Middle & Small-Lot Zoning Package

Update zoning to encourage townhouses, duplexes,

quadplexes, and small-lot homes to expand housing

options and improve affordability.

Missing middle housing provides options for residents

who may not be served by traditional single-family

homes or large-scale multi-family developments. These

housing types, including duplexes, townhomes, cottage

courts, and accessory dwellings units (ADUs), can fit

within existing neighborhood contexts while increasing

housing choice and flexibility.

Today, Plain City allows some of these formats only

conditionally, while others, such as 3-4 unit building

and small-scale multi-family, are more restricted. Larger

minimum lot sizes and relatively low maximum lot

coverage in several districts also limit the feasibility of

smaller, more attainable housing types.

Plain City should evaluate its zoning code to reduce

these barriers and introduce targeted updates that

expand where and how missing middle units can be

built. This may include reducing minimum lot sizes,

increasing allowable lot coverage, simplifying approvals

for duplexes and ADUs, and permitting small multi-unit

buildings in appropriate areas.

Clear design standards and examples should accompany

these updates to ensure new development remains

compatible with the Village’s character while supporting

a more diverse and attainable housing supply.

Definitions:

Small Lot Zoning Packages: Small lot zoning packages

are typically introduced to allow higher density within

a single lot. This results in increased housing supply and

density as well as affordability. Components of a package

include the allowing of accessory dwelling units (ADUs),

reduced minimum lot size requirements, increasing allowed

density (for example, duplexes and ADUs), reduced setback

requirements, and size maximums. Ultimately, this can spur

infill within a given area.

78 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Today, Plain City has a limited supply of missing-middle

housing. During the first Community Open House,

residents were asked to select images that reflect the

types of housing they would like to see in the Village.

This input will help to shape the preferred housing types

and styles for the future.

Missing Middle Housing: The range of medium-density,

house-scale residential buildings that bridge the gap between

single-family homes and larger multi-family developments.

These housing types include duplexes, triplexes, townhomes,

and courtyard apartments, designed to be compatible in scale

and character with traditional single-family neighborhoods

while offering more diverse and attainable housing options.

Missing middle housing supports walkable communities,

expands housing affordability, and responds to evolving

demographic and lifestyle needs. Missing middle housing also

provides more affordable and attainable options for younger

families, smaller households, and older adults. Zoning codes

can be adapted to accommodate more of this type of housing.

2.4 Uptown Living Initiative

Encourage new housing in Uptown to support local

businesses and increase daily activity.

Expanding housing opportunities in Uptown can help

reinforce its role as the heart of the community while

creating a unique living experience not found elsewhere

in the Village. Living in Uptown offers a blend of urban

convenience and small-town charm, where residents can

walk to shops, restaurants, parks, and community events

while remaining connected to a close-knit community.

Additional residents within walking distance of local

destinations help support small businesses, increase foot

traffic, and create a more active and vibrant environment

throughout the day and evening. This consistent presence

of people not only strengthens the local economy but

also enhances safety, energy, and overall sense of place.

The Village should encourage upper-story residential,

small-scale infill, and mixed-use development that

integrates housing into the existing Uptown fabric.

This includes identifying opportunity sites, supporting

adaptive reuse of existing buildings, and ensuring new

development contributes to a walkable, engaging

public realm.

By creating more opportunities to live in Uptown, Plain

City can foster an active, connected lifestyle where

daily needs are within reach, and community life is

experienced just outside the front door.
