DOWNTOWN IMPROVEMENT GRANT

City of Columbus, IN

The City of Columbus, Indiana established a

Downtown Improvement Grant to support

reinvestment and upgrades within its downtown

core. Eligible properties must be located within

the Downtown Improvement Area, and have

commercial activity as its primary purpose. This

includes retail and specialty shops, services,

restaurants, bars, live entertainment, cultural

venues, galleries, and professional offices. Grants

are for long term improvements, which include:

interior demolition, permanent equipment,

permanent lighting, flooring, windows/doors,

historic restoration, permanent interior walls,

permanent counter/point of sale, architectural

permitting fees, ADA compliance, and lead

remediation. The City is matching up to $15,000

for small to medium projects, and $50,000 for

larger projects.

80 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

2.5 Uptown Neighborhood Reinvestment Program

Support reinvestment, redevelopment, and infrastructure

improvements in and around Uptown neighborhoods.

As Plain City continues to grow, reinvestment in

existing neighborhoods, particularly those surrounding

Uptown, will be critical to maintaining housing

quality and neighborhood stability. These areas play an

important role in the community’s identity and provide

opportunities for incremental improvement and infill.

The Village should explore programs and tools that

support property maintenance, rehabilitation, and

infrastructure upgrades, including sidewalks, utilities, and

streetscapes. Targeted assistance programs, partnerships,

or incentives can help homeowners and property owners

reinvest in aging housing stock.

This effort should also identify opportunities for

compatible infill development on underutilized or

vacant parcels, ensuring that new housing fits within

the existing neighborhood context. By prioritizing

reinvestment alongside new growth, Plain City can

support long-term neighborhood health and a more

balanced housing strategy.

2.6 Materials & Design Standards

Ensure consistent application of building quality,

architectural character, and site design standards across

the Village.

Plain City has established design standards and Uptown

design guidelines that reflect the community’s desired

character, including high-quality materials, pedestrian

oriented storefronts, and cohesive architectural detailing.

As new housing types and development patterns emerge,

the Village should focus on aligning and consistently

applying these existing standards across both Uptown

and newer growth areas. Cohesive signage and design

standards should also be developed and consistently

applied to commercial and industrial development.

Design standards should be clear and predictable

to developers while remaining flexible enough to

accommodate a range of housing types and commercial

building styles. Periodic updates and user-friendly

formats can help ensure the standards remain relevant,

easy to navigate, and consistently applied.

By strengthening coordination between existing

guidelines and future development, Plain City can

maintain a cohesive identity and ensure that new

neighborhoods, retail buildings, and office and industrial

facilities reflect a consistent level of quality and attention

to design.

Source: MKSK

DESIGN GUIDELINES

City of Bexley, OH

In 2002, the City of Bexley adopted its Main

Street Design Guidelines, developed by MKSK,

to strengthen the visual identity and functional

quality of its primary commercial corridor. The

guidelines established clear, citywide expectations

for building form, architectural character,

streetscape elements, and pedestrian amenities,

ensuring that new development and reinvestment

supported a cohesive, walkable Main Street

environment.

For more than twenty years, Bexley has relied

on these guidelines to shape incremental

redevelopment, façade improvements, and public

realm enhancements.

STRENGTHEN OUR HOMES & NEIGHBORHOODS | RECOMMENDATIONS 81

Create a stronger local

job base and business

ecosystem

Strengthening Plain City’s local economy is essential

to balancing the Village’s rapid residential growth

and supporting its long-term fiscal health. As the

community continues to attract new residents, there is

a clear opportunity to evolve from a primarily bedroom

community, in which most residents commute outside

the Village to work, into a more complete “live-work”

environment. By growing local jobs, attracting essential

big-box style development, and supporting small

businesses in strategic locations, Plain City can capture

more employment, retain spending locally, and build a

stronger, more resilient economic base.

Today, most Plain City residents commute elsewhere

for work and shopping amenities, limiting the Village’s

ability to fully benefit from its population growth. While

this pattern reflects strong regional connectivity and

access to major job centers, it also places pressure on local

services without a corresponding increase in income tax

revenue. At the same time, job growth within the Village

has not kept pace with housing development, and the

types of jobs available do not always align with the skills

and incomes of residents. Addressing this imbalance is

critical to ensuring that growth translates into long-term

community benefit.

Creating more opportunities for residents to live and

work locally will require a targeted and intentional

approach. This includes supporting small businesses,

encouraging entrepreneurship, and attracting employers

in sectors that complement the Village’s character and

regional position while filling gaps in the retail market.

82 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

A ceremony celebrating the owner of Yoder’s True Value

hardware store for 40 years of service to the Plain

City community.

Strategic locations, such as key corridors, employment

areas, and Uptown can serve as hubs for economic

activity, offering spaces for businesses to grow while

enhancing the overall vibrancy of the community.

Balancing residential growth with local employment and

business activity is what allows Plain City to continue

investing in streets, utilities, parks, and public safety over

time. By aligning economic development with land use,

infrastructure, and community priorities, the Village can

strengthen its tax base, support high-quality services, and

create a more complete and connected community. The

recommendations in this section provide a framework for

growing jobs and businesses in a way that supports long

term sustainability and reinforces Plain City’s identity as

a place to both live and work.

GROW JOBS & LOCAL BUSINESS

Conditions & Trends

96%

Residents working

outside Plain City

Very few people both work and live in Plain City. Of

the 2,257 employed Plain City residents, 96% commute

outside the Village for work, and only 8.3% of local

jobs are filled by residents. This signifies that job growth

has not kept pace with residential growth, and local

wages remain lower than what local residents earn in

surrounding communities.

Educational attainment among Plain City’s residents

is generally higher than that of its workforce. 34.3%

hold a bachelor’s degree or higher, compared to just

16.6% of the local workforce. This gap reflects how

residential growth has attracted highly educated

residents who often work elsewhere, while local

employment opportunities remain limited in high-skill,

high-paying positions.

Regionally, Plain City is surrounded by emerging

employment and industry sectors. Many companies are

establishing logistics facilities, manufacturing, and data

centers in Central Ohio. Most of these job centers are

within a 45-minute commute from Plain City. Examples

include Intel in Licking County, Amazon Web Services

in Union County, retail distribution centers in West

Jefferson, electric vehicles investments in Fayette County,

and Anduril in Pickaway County.

"We welcome

growth and new

businesses - they

are much needed

here!"

While Columbus accounts for the largest share of

Plain City’s workforce, the majority come from a wide

range of surrounding townships. 14.1% of workers

commute from Columbus, but 80% commute from

various townships across Central Ohio.

The education gap between residents and the local

workforce is widening.  Since 2010, the share of

residents with a bachelor’s degree or higher has increased

by 36%. Over the same period, the share of the local

workforce with a bachelor’s degree or higher has nearly

halved, dropping by 41% relative to 2010 levels. Rising

home prices may be exacerbating this existing gap.

Most of Plain City’s operating budget comes from

income taxes. Making up 70% of the Village’s operating

budget, this underscores the importance of attracting

more jobs to the Village to ensure that revenue is

proportionate to population growth.

RECOMMENDATIONS

GROW JOBS & LOCAL BUSINESS

These recommendations guide how Plain City grows its local economy to better balance

residential growth with jobs and business activity. They focus on supporting small

businesses, attracting employers, and creating vibrant places where people can work,

shop, and gather. By aligning economic development with land use, infrastructure, and

community priorities, the Village can strengthen its tax base, expand local opportunities,

and create a “live-work” community that supports long-term economic sustainability.

Potential next steps:

• Conduct a recurring competitiveness audit every

2–3 years, comparing incentives, approval timelines,

annexation tools, site readiness, and utilities against

peer communities.

• Create target-industry value proposition sheets with

concise, sector-specific materials using current data

on land, access, workforce, and available tools.

• Maintain a live strategic site inventory including

acreage, ownership, zoning, utilities, access,

constraints, readiness, and likely industry fit.

• Track prospect and project outcomes, including

inquiries, wins/losses, decision factors, incentive use,

and realized results.

• Align annexation strategy with infrastructure and job

targets by periodically reviewing relevant programs

and agreements.

• Coordinate local tools with regional partners so

Plain City’s package complements broader state and

regional offerings.

WHY INCENTIVIZE DEVELOPMENT?

Plain City should not incentivize every project,

and it should not do so automatically. But for

job-producing development, incentives can be

a practical tool to help the Village compete for

businesses that strengthen the local economy

and expand the municipal tax base. The Village’s

own incentive policy makes this point clearly:

incentives are intended to attract businesses that

invest in facilities, create jobs and payroll, and

contribute to long-term community prosperity.

The policy also emphasizes that incentives should

favor projects that generate new full-time jobs,

higher-paying jobs, and substantial additions to

the local tax base.

That matters because Plain City is competing

in a real global market. Business attraction

decisions are shaped by land readiness, utilities,

workforce access, timing, infrastructure, and

overall deal economics. Ohio’s economic

development messaging reflects these realities,

pairing incentives with site readiness, workforce

support, and speed to market to win projects over

competing communities.

For residents, the reason to use incentives is

straightforward: successful job-producing

development can help fund the places and

services that make Plain City stronger. The

Village states that its main revenue source is a

1.5% income tax, and that growth in income tax

revenue helps support rising service demands,

staffing, and long-delayed or newly needed public

projects. In other words, when incentives help

secure the right business investment, they can

support reinvestment in infrastructure, amenities,

Uptown, and neighborhoods over time.

The key, then, is not whether to incentivize

development at all. It is whether Plain City is

using incentives selectively, transparently, and in

ways that produce clear public value. The Village’s

job is to stay competitive for the right projects

while ensuring the return shows up in jobs,

payroll, infrastructure, and visible community

benefit.

GROW JOBS & LOCAL BUSINESSES | RECOMMENDATIONS 85

3.2 Implement the Creekview Commerce Park Plan

Creekview Commerce Park is one of the Village’s clearest opportunities to convert regional economic

momentum into lasting local benefit. As Central Ohio continues to grow, Plain City is facing increasing

pressure for new housing and development. The Village has also made clear that long-term fiscal health

depends on more than residential expansion alone. Plain City’s own public guidance notes that maintaining

a balanced, financially sustainable community requires attracting industrial development that creates jobs

with living wages, and that the community has historically lacked sufficient sites for this kind of growth.

Creekview begins to address that gap.

The Creekview Commerce Park should therefore be treated as more than a future development site.

It should be advanced as a master-planned business park with the capacity to diversify the Village’s

economic base, provide shovel-ready sites, attract right-fit employers, and create new opportunities for

both local residents and the broader community. The Village has already framed the project in these terms,

emphasizing its potential to capitalize on recent large regional projects, align with local and state target

industries, and generate substantial income tax revenue for Plain City.

Implementation should remain closely tied to the qualities that distinguish Creekview from a conventional

industrial project. The Village and its partners should continue to position the site as a high-quality, well

buffered employment district with strong development controls, durable design expectations, meaningful

open space preservation, and a clear commitment to compatibility with nearby properties and the Big Darby

Creek landscape. That includes following through on the project’s intended campus-like character, its

extensive edge buffering, its internal trail and amenity framework, and its emphasis on attracting businesses

that are a long-term fit for Plain City rather than simply filling land quickly.

Just as important, Creekview should be pursued

with a clear municipal value framework. Success

should not be measured only by buildings

constructed. It should be measured by the

extent to which the project expands payroll,

increases the number of quality jobs within

Village boundaries, supports complementary

commercial services, and improves Plain

City’s long-term fiscal capacity. The Village’s

incentive policy already points in this direction

by prioritizing projects that create jobs, produce

payroll, and add meaningfully to the local tax

base.

In that sense, Creekview is not simply

a development project. It is a strategic

implementation district that can help Plain The Village at Hendrix in Conway, AR demonstrates a

City capture more of the upside of regional walkable, mixed-use model integrating employment,

growth while maintaining stronger control over housing, retail, and green space, supporting job

design, infrastructure, and long-term community creation, economic activity, and a vibrant, amenity-rich

outcomes. The project’s scale, location, and environment. Source: DPZ Design

evolving entitlement status make it one of the

most important economic development actions

in the Plan Update.

Why Jobs Matter Fiscally in Plain City

The Village’s primary source of revenue is a 1.5% income

tax that generates about $3.8 million annually, covering

roughly 70% of the operating budget.

In Ohio, municipalities do not operate like counties,

townships, or school districts. Their most important

locally controlled revenue source is often the municipal

income tax, which means that attracting and retaining

jobs inside municipal boundaries can have an outsized

impact on long-term fiscal capacity. For Plain City, that

makes employment growth more than an economic

development objective. It is also a core fiscal strategy.

Other municipal revenue sources matter too. Property

tax revenues support local government in more limited Imperial Business Park, Source: The Silverman Group

and structured ways than many residents assume, and

municipalities typically share that tax base with schools,

counties, and other jurisdictions. Development-related

revenues such as permit fees, plan review fees, utility

connection charges, and impact fees can help offset the

cost of growth, but they are often episodic rather than

long-term operating revenue. Municipalities may also

use tools such as community reinvestment areas, tax

increment financing, special assessments, and payment

in lieu of taxes (PILOT)-style arrangements to shape

fiscal outcomes or recover value from development under

certain conditions.

The practical point for Plain City is simple: not all

growth contributes to the Village in the same way. Land

uses that generate substantial employment and payroll Van Aken District, Source: MKSK

can strengthen the income tax base and help support

services, infrastructure, and reinvestment over time. That

is why this Plan Update places a strong emphasis on

jobs attraction, business retention, and careful alignment

of annexation and incentive policy with the Village’s

long-term fiscal interests. The Village’s annexation

incentive framework already points in this direction and

can be sharpened further through job-oriented policy

alignment.

Grandview Yard, Source: MKSK

CREEKVIEW COMMERCE PARK

Creekview Commerce Park Site, Source: Google Earth

Defining Success at Creekview

1. Creekview broadens Plain City’s job base in a

measurable way. The district attracts employers in

target sectors that generate substantial new payroll,

quality jobs, and long-term income tax value for

the Village rather than low-yield or short-lived

activity.

2. The site functions as a true mixed employment

district, not a one-note industrial park.  While

employment uses remain primary, the overall plan

accommodates a thoughtful mix of business and

supporting commercial uses across sub-areas so the

district can adapt to market demand and maximize

the utility of a large, strategic site.

3. The Village can tell a clear annual “net gain”

story. Creekview is tracked over time using

meaningful measures such as jobs created, payroll

generated, wages per acre, capital investment,

taxable value, infrastructure costs, and net fiscal

return, allowing leadership to evaluate performance

beyond ribbon cuttings alone.

4. The project conserves and organizes open space

as a defining feature, not leftover land. Large

internal open spaces, creek-edge protections,

88 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

513.9 acres

wooded areas, wetlands, and peripheral buffers

are preserved and integrated into the district

framework in a way that supports both ecological

function and long-term development quality.

5. Creekview demonstrates best-in-class watershed

stewardship.  Within the Big Darby Creek

watershed, development is guided by careful

stormwater planning, low-impact design, and

durable best management practices such as rain

gardens, bioswales, permeable paving, naturalized

detention features, and other methods that reduce

runoff and mitigate impervious surface impacts.

6. Its streets and infrastructure perform well

and enhance its cohesive district brand and

aesthetics. New streets are not merely access

drives. They support the land use framework

functionally while delivering a high-quality public

realm through coordinated trees, landscaping,

sidewalks, pathways, signage, and lighting that

establish Creekview as a premier district and a

positive front door to the Village.

7. Internal connectivity is integrated from the

beginning. T Trails, sidewalks, passive open spaces,

and shared amenities help workers and visitors move

comfortably through the district, strengthen its

campus-like setting, and make the site more than a

collection of isolated buildings.

8. The district contributes to a larger trail and open

space network. Creekview’s internal paths and

passive spaces are designed to connect over time to

emerging local and regional trail ambitions, helping

tie the district into Plain City’s broader mobility,

recreation, and greenway vision.

9. Higher-intensity uses are placed where they make

the most sense. Th The most intense employment and

logistics functions are directed toward the center of

the site or other appropriate locations, while edges

are carefully managed to protect adjacent properties,

sensitive environmental areas, and the overall image

of the district.

10. Design quality remains consistently high across

phases and users. Buildings, site layouts, screening,

fencing, loading areas, signage, and landscape

treatment all reinforce a common standard so that

the district reads as intentional, well-kept, and

compatible with Plain City’s expectations over time.

11. Creekview becomes a gateway district. Th The

northern edge and other key entries are designed

as gateway moments that signal arrival into both

a distinct employment district and the Village

itself, using landscape design, signage, lighting,

and coordinated frontage improvements to create a

strong first impression.

12. The district is evaluated as an enduring

economic asset. Plain City maintains a disciplined

performance record for Creekview and uses it to

inform future incentives, infrastructure decisions,

marketing, and policy updates, ensuring the district

continues to deliver community value over time.

The Creekview Commerce Park Vision

The Creekview site is envisioned as a high-quality,

commerce park that delivers more jobs, higher payroll,

and long-term fiscal benefit for Plain City. A flexible mix

of employment uses is paired with conserved open space,

strong buffers, and Big Darby Creek-sensitive design.

High-quality infrastructure, internal trails, and worker

amenities create a campus-like setting, while targeted

industries and clear performance tracking ensure lasting

economic value and community return.

3.3 Develop a Revenue Capture Framework for

Capital-Intensive Uses with Limited Job Creation

Before accommodating uses characterized by low

employment density and high land and infrastructure

demand, such as data centers, Plain City should establish

a flexible revenue-capture framework that ensures these

projects produce clear and lasting public value.

Ohio communities have several tools that can be

layered to recover value from major private investment

even when direct job creation is relatively limited.

Under Ohio’s TIF statutes, municipalities may redirect

service payments in lieu of taxes toward eligible public

infrastructure improvements, and those payments are

collected in the same manner as real property taxes. Ohio

law also allows a New Community Authority to impose

a community development charge that is collected with

property taxes and can become a lien on the property if

unpaid. New Albany’s Community Authority provides

a clear example of how this type of charge can be used

as part of a broader local funding structure: its charge is

based on assessed valuation, collected by the county with

property taxes, and may not exceed 9.75 mills.

This matters because some high-investment uses can

bring extraordinary capital expenditure while producing

relatively few on-site jobs. Ohio’s computer data center

tax exemption reflects that dynamic: eligibility is driven

by at least $100 million in capital investment and at least

$1.5 million in annual compensation at the site. For a

community like Plain City, that creates an important

policy question. If a project is likely to consume

large amounts of land, utility capacity, infrastructure

investment, and long-term public attention, the Village

should not rely on conventional job-creation incentives

alone to determine whether the project is worthwhile.

Accordingly, Plain City should explore a specialized

framework that allows it to assemble the right mix

of cost-recovery and revenue-producing tools on a

case-by-case basis. That framework may include TIF

service payments, district-based charges, negotiated

infrastructure participation, compensation agreements,

and other mechanisms permitted under Ohio law. The

purpose is not to pre-select one tool today, but to ensure

the Village has a flexible and legally sound structure

available when considering exceptional projects that are

capital-heavy but employment-light.

94 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Just as important, the Village should be clear about

its posture: Plain City should not actively pursue or

approve employment-light, land-heavy uses in strategic

growth areas unless an appropriate revenue-capture and

infrastructure cost-recovery framework is already in place

or can be secured as part of the development agreement.

Without that framework, these projects risk consuming

valuable land and public capacity without delivering a

commensurate return to residents in the form of revenue,

infrastructure, amenities, or broader community benefit.

A well-structured local model would help the Village

evaluate such projects more fairly and more transparently.

It would allow Plain City to ask not only whether a

project is large, but whether it generates a meaningful

net gain after accounting for infrastructure demands,

utility burden, land consumption, and the opportunity

cost of foreclosing other job-producing uses. In that way,

the recommendation supports the broader goals already

embedded in the Plan Update: fiscal resilience, strategic

land use, infrastructure stewardship, and a stronger long

term return from growth.

Potential next steps:

• Review tools that can support revenue capture from

capital-intensive, low-employment uses, including

TIF service payments, New Community Authority

charges, and negotiated development agreements.

• Develop criteria for when a specialized framework

is required, based on factors such as jobs per

acre, infrastructure burden, utility demand, land

consumption, and strategic site value.

• Prepare a model negotiation framework that

identifies which costs or community benefits must be

addressed before approving such projects.

• Coordinate legal and financial review in advance

so the Village can respond quickly and consistently

when exceptional proposals arise.

• Establish a policy position that strategic employment

lands should remain prioritized for higher-job and

higher-payroll uses unless an alternative project can

demonstrate a strong and durable net public return.

RECOMMENDATIONS 95

3.4 Plan for transition zones between incompatible

uses

Establish clear transition strategies where industrial,

commercial, and residential areas meet to reduce

conflicts and protect long-term value on all sides.

This should include careful attention to land use

patterning, site design, access management, buffering,

landscaping, lighting, and noise mitigation. Gateway

Commercial areas are especially important locations for

this “feathering” approach, helping the Village manage

change while maintaining compatibility between new

investment and established neighborhoods.

3.5 A Avoid scattering commercial land uses

Focus future commercial growth in intentional nodes

and corridors rather than allowing it to disperse broadly

across the community. A more concentrated pattern

supports infrastructure efficiency, reinforces market

visibility, and improves the Village’s ability to create

successful destinations over time. This direction also

aligns with the Creekview strategy and broader efforts

to match land use decisions with long-term service and

fiscal efficiency.

3.6 Develop cohesive signage & design standards for

employment areas

Create a consistent family of signage, landscape, and

development design standards across commercial and

industrial areas to reinforce identity, improve wayfinding,

and elevate the quality of new investment. Over time,

this could evolve into a more detailed sub-branding

effort for key districts and gateways, helping Plain City

present a clear and coordinated image to businesses,

residents, and visitors.

3.7 Identify and respond to barriers to workforce

availability

Work with regional, county, and institutional partners

to better understand the barriers that limit workforce

access, including transportation, housing affordability,

childcare, and training connections. This work should

position the Village to align local actions with broader

regional strategies and leverage existing programs

where possible. Tolles and other education or workforce

partners may have a meaningful role in connecting

residents to future employment opportunities.

96 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

3.8 Build an inventory & strategy for underutilized

properties

Develop a land and building inventory to identify

underutilized, vacant, or strategically important

properties that may warrant public attention, partnership,

or intervention. This should include awareness of

sites that are unattractive to the private market due to

location, condition, or prior use, but that may create

long-term drag on surrounding neighborhoods if left

unaddressed. The Village should explore tools such

as land bank coordination, housing partnerships, and

targeted redevelopment partnerships to reposition these

assets over time.

3.9 Explore joint economic development efforts with

neighboring jurisdictions

Explore joint economic development agreements and

cooperative projects with neighboring communities

where shared infrastructure, access, workforce, or

tax-base considerations suggest mutual benefit. A

collaborative approach can help Plain City compete

more effectively for strategic investment while reducing

unnecessary duplication and jurisdictional friction.

Today, Uptown features a variety of signage types

across its businesses. As outlined in Recommendation

3.6, future efforts could focus on integrating more

cohesive design standards to create a unified identity

and improve wayfinding.

RECOMMENDATIONS 96

3.10 Prepare for the economic development

implications of passenger rail in Dublin

Monitor and plan for the long-term economic

development implications of potential passenger rail

investment in nearby Dublin and the broader northwest

growth corridor. While the infrastructure may not

be located in Plain City, the Village could experience

meaningful secondary effects related to access, land value,

development pressure, tourism, and business attraction.

Planning early will help the Village position itself to

respond strategically as this conversation evolves.

3.11 Coordinate land use & capital planning with

utility partners

Strengthen ongoing coordination with utility providers

so that land use decisions, annexation strategy,

growth areas, and capital improvements are aligned.

Utility readiness, timing, and cost will continue to

shape economic development potential, making this

coordination essential to both prospect strategy and

long-range planning.

3.12 Prioritize investment areas & align public

spending accordingly

Define priority investment areas where Village actions,

infrastructure improvements, and public spending should

be focused to support desired economic development

outcomes. This helps ensure that capital decisions, public

realm upgrades, utility expansions, and other investments

reinforce the Village’s development strategy rather than

working at cross-purposes.

3.13 Leverage natural & rural assets to support

tourism

Build on assets such as the Big Darby Creek, the rural

landscape, and Plain City’s agricultural character to

expand tourism and the visitor economy. This should

include considering how recreation, scenic quality, trails,

events, and destination-oriented business activity can

work together to strengthen local identity and generate

complementary economic activity. This aligns well with

the Village’s broader connectivity and placemaking goals.

such as healthcare and lodging

Identify and support opportunities for new healthcare

services, hospitality, and other missing commercial

amenities that would improve quality of life, strengthen

the Village’s market position, and better serve residents,

MIDWEST CONNECT PASSENGER RAIL

Dublin, Ohio

A new Amtrak line, the Midwest Connect, is in

the service development planning stage, likely to

begin passenger service in the next 6-10 years.

The line will connect Chicago and Pittsburgh,

passing through Central Ohio. In response to

this project, the City of Dublin and Mid-Ohio

Regional Planning Commission are working

together to proactively plan a future passenger rail

station and surrounding development, advancing a

vision for transit-oriented growth and multimodal

connectivity.

Located just two miles from the Village's eastern

boundary, this project is significant for Plain

City. A passenger rail station will greatly increase

regional accessibility, attract new residents and

businesses, and position the Village to capture

spillover growth and attract major employers.

To capitalize on this opportunity, Plain City can

invest in strengthening connections to Dublin via

road, trail, or future transit links to position itself

as an accessible place for residents and businesses

seeking proximity to regional rail access.

workers, and visitors. These uses may not emerge

everywhere on their own, so the Village should be clear

about where such investment is most appropriate and

how it can help support it. Utility readiness, timing,

and cost will continue to shape economic development

potential, making this coordination essential to both

prospect strategy and long-range planning.

Provide a well

distributed, connected

system of parks, trails, and

open spaces

Expanding and caring for Plain City’s parks and open

spaces is essential to maintaining the community’s

quality of life as it grows. Parks, trails, and natural areas

are where the Village’s health, identity, and landscape

come together in everyday life, providing space for

recreation, connection, and community events. As Plain

City continues to welcome new residents, the Village

will build on this foundation by expanding a connected

system of parks and open spaces, anchored by Pastime

Park, new neighborhood and community parks, and

the Big Darby Creek. This approach ensures that every

neighborhood has access to high-quality open space as

growth continues.

Today, Pastime Park serves as the heart of the

community’s park system, hosting events, recreation,

and daily activities. While this central role reflects

its importance, it also highlights the need to expand

and diversify the system. As new development occurs,

particularly in areas south of Main Street, the demand

for park space, recreational amenities, and event

programming will continue to increase. Relying too

heavily on a single destination can limit access, create

overuse, and reduce the overall effectiveness of the

system in serving a growing population.

A more connected and distributed network of parks and

trails will help meet these needs while reinforcing the

Village’s character. New neighborhood parks, expanded

trail connections, and preservation of natural features,

98 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

The Plain City Aquatic Center, opened in 2012, caters to

depth entry areas.

especially along Big Darby Creek, can provide all

residents with accessible, walkable options for recreation

and relaxation. These investments not only support

physical and mental well-being but also strengthen

environmental stewardship and protect the natural assets

that define Plain City’s idyllic setting.

By expanding access, improving connectivity, and

maintaining existing assets, Plain City can ensure its

parks system grows alongside the community. A well

planned network of parks and open spaces supports

everyday life, enhances neighborhood value, and

creates lasting places for gathering and recreation. The

recommendations in this section provide a framework for

building and sustaining a recreation network that reflects

the needs, identity, and future of Plain City.

Acres of existing and

More than half of Plain City’s residents live within a

15-minute walk of a park, reflecting strong access to

green space. Currently, 58% of the population meets this

threshold, and access is expected to improve further as

newly proposed parks are completed.

The Village is rapidly expanding its recreation network.

Recently completed projects include the Jackson Street

Dog Park, McKitrick Nature Preserve, and Smucker

Park. Additional parks and recreation spaces are

proposed or underway at the former RANCO industrial

site and Raber Farm in the northern part of the Village.

Plain City’s location within the Big Darby Creek

watershed provides unique access to one of Ohio’s

most ecologically significant natural corridors.

The Big Darby Creek and its tributaries shape the

Village’s landscape and conservation approach, offering

opportunities to expand a connected system of parks,

trails, and protected open space anchored by this

waterway.

expanded on-road and shared use paths throughout

planned developments.

planning with growth, infrastructure, and conservation priorities, Plain City can ensure

that all residents have access to high-quality open space while preserving the natural

features that define the community’s character and quality of life.

The RANCO site operated from 1952 to 2004 as a

manufacturing facility for thermostat, HVAC, and

automotive parts. Once operations ceased, the site

underwent a years-long environmental cleanup process

to address damage caused by decades of industrial

use. Remediation efforts included the removal of

contaminated soils, installation of caps and soil barriers

safeguards will guide future activity, ensuring that the

property can safely transition to a new use.

Led by Burgess and Niple, the 94-acre Whittier

Peninsula was redeveloped from a large industrial

brownfield to a signature nature respite on the edge

of Downtown Columbus. Once a landfill, industrial

park, impound lot, and rail yard, it was hard to imagine

that the site would become the signature destination

it is today. Though a master plan for the site was

completed in 2005, significant brownfield remediation

was required before any development could begin. To

conduct brownfield assessment and cleanup, the City

was awarded $1.5 million in grant funding and Metro

Parks entered the property into the Ohio Environmental

Protection Agency Voluntary Action Program.

Today, the park is a valuable asset in the heart of

Columbus, complete with an 18,000-square foot nature

center, climbing wall, sand volleyball court, obstacle

course, and dog park along with vast green space and

wetlands. Key partnerships, major funding wins, and a

bold community vision were all critical in establishing

Scioto Audubon Metro Park.

EXPAND & CARE FOR OUR PARKS | RECOMMENDATIONS 101

Monon Trail, Source: Travel Indiana

Carmel Access Bikeways, Source: Little Vignettes Photo

GREENWAY NETWORK

Carmel, IN

The Carmel Access Bikeways project, set forth

in 2008, included eight city bike routes and five

community bike loops. Loops are named for the

specific area they serve and connect to a variety

of local amenities. The eight longer east-west and

north-south routes connect bikers across the city.

Loops range in length from 6.8 to 13.9 miles,

encompassing shopping districts, neighborhoods,

schools, city streets, Downtown Carmel, and the

White River.

These trails are paved and aimed toward casual

riders. Along the loops, several “trailheads"

feature information kiosks, benches, trash

cans, and restrooms. In a city with no public

transportation, the interconnected cycling

network provides an alternative to taking a car.

102 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

4.2 Deliver a New South Community Park

(Sweeney Run)

Establish a signature community park and greenway at

Sweeney Run that supports future development while

strengthening access, recreation, and walkable and

bikeable connections to Uptown.

Sweeney Run presents a unique opportunity for

Plain City to expand its park system while enhancing

connectivity in the southern portion of the Village.

Located south of Main Street along West Avenue, the

site offers space for a linear greenway that can link new

residential and commercial areas to Uptown, creating

a safe and attractive corridor for walking, biking, and

passive recreation.

As a community-focused greenway, Sweeney Run can

provide trails, seating, and natural spaces that encourage

casual recreation and neighborhood interaction. Rather

than hosting large active fields, the park should prioritize

walking paths, shaded seating areas, and connections

to surrounding neighborhoods, supporting both daily

movement and leisurely enjoyment of the outdoors.

Linkages to Uptown and surrounding development are

central to the park’s value. Well-designed pedestrian

and bicycle trails will enhance access, foster active

transportation, and strengthen community cohesion.

Coordinated planning with property owners, Village

staff, and developers will ensure these connections are

feasible, safe, and aligned with broader Village growth

goals and capacities.

A comprehensive focus area plan will guide park

layout, phased implementation, funding priorities, and

maintenance strategies. Community input will play a

key role, allowing residents to provide feedback on trail

alignment, seating, amenities, and design features that

reflect Plain City’s character and values.

At its heart, Sweeney Run is more than a linear park; it

is a strategic greenway investment that supports future

development, strengthens active and passive recreation

opportunities, and connects residents to the heart of

Uptown. Thoughtful planning and implementation will

create a lasting asset that enhances Plain City’s livability,

encourages outdoor activity, and reinforces the Village’s

identity as a connected and welcoming community.

4.3 Pastime Park & Bicentennial Park Reinvestment

Reinvest in Pastime Park and Bicentennial Park to

enhance amenities, connectivity, and everyday usability

for residents of all ages.

As two of Plain City’s most well-used and recognizable

parks, Pastime Park and Bicentennial Park represent

the core of the community’s recreational life. Continued

reinvestment should focus on updating aging amenities,

improving accessibility, and enhancing connections

within and between the parks and surrounding

neighborhoods. Opportunities may include upgraded

playgrounds, improved pedestrian pathways, expanded

seating and gathering areas, and enhanced wayfinding.

Strengthening these parks as welcoming, flexible, and

easy-to-use spaces will help ensure they continue to

serve residents of all ages while accommodating growing

demand and evolving recreational needs.

EXPAND & CARE FOR OUR PARKS | RECOMMENDATIONS 103

4.4 Neighborhood Parks Network

Ensure equitable distribution and safe access to parks

across the Village.

As Plain City grows, expanding and better connecting

its network of neighborhood parks will be essential

to maintaining quality of life and equitable access

to recreation. This effort should focus on identifying

gaps in park service areas, particularly in emerging

residential neighborhoods, and ensuring that residents

can safely walk or bike to nearby parks. Emphasis should

be placed on integrating parks with sidewalks, trails,

and greenways, while also considering small, flexible

park spaces and larger parks with desired amenities

that can be incorporated into new development. A

more connected and accessible system will help bring

recreation closer to home and support a healthier, more

active community.

4.5 Parks Master Plan & Conceptual Plans for Select

Parks

Define long-term priorities for park investments and

create site designs for key priority sites.

As Plain City continues to grow, a comprehensive Parks

Master Plan will help guide strategic investment in

the park system and ensure resources are distributed

effectively. This effort should identify community

priorities, evaluate existing park conditions, and establish

a clear framework for future improvements, expansions,

and new park development. Conceptual plans for select

priority sites such as future parks or major reinvestment

areas can provide more detailed direction, helping the

Village visualize opportunities, coordinate funding, and

move projects from idea to implementation.

4.6 Park Operations & Maintenance Master Plan

Establish staffing, funding, and maintenance standards

that prioritize the safety and quality of parks.

Maintaining high-quality parks requires a strategic and

sustainable approach to operations, staffing, and funding.

Developing an Operations and Maintenance Master

Plan will help the Village define service standards,

identify resource needs, and ensure parks are safe, clean,

and well-maintained as the system expands. This effort

should consider staffing levels, equipment needs, lifecycle

replacement planning, and funding strategies, while also

establishing consistent maintenance expectations across

all park facilities. A proactive approach will help protect

past investments and support a positive experience for

residents and visitors alike.

4.7 T Trails & Greenway Spine

Build a comprehensive trail and greenway network that

links neighborhoods, parks, and Uptown.

A connected trail and greenway network is a

foundational element of Plain City’s vision for a

healthy, active, and accessible community. By linking

neighborhoods, parks, schools, and Uptown, this system

will support recreation, alternative transportation, and

environmental stewardship across the Village while

improving access to everyday destinations.

The network should prioritize connections that link

key community assets and regional destinations, while

addressing gaps in existing sidewalks, bike facilities,

and off-road trails. Particular attention should be given

to creating safe and comfortable crossings along major

corridors, ensuring that barriers such as high-traffic

roadways or waterways do not limit accessibility. Trail

design should incorporate a mix of multi-use paved

paths, soft-surface trails, bike lanes, and walking routes

balancing usability, safety, and environmental sensitivity.

Where feasible, leveraging existing corridors, such as

utility easements, stream corridors, and public rights-of

way, can expand connectivity while minimizing costs and

environmental impacts. Trail planning and construction

should be done in coordination with Village partners to

ensure seamless connectivity throughout the Village and

beyond.

As growth continues, the Village should integrate trail

development into new subdivisions and site plans,

working with developers to extend the network and

secure key connections over time. Building the trail

and greenway spine should be phased strategically,

focusing first on segments that close critical gaps,

connect to Uptown, and serve the greatest number of

residents. Each segment should incorporate wayfinding,

streetscape elements, seating, and safe crossings to create

a welcoming and functional user experience.

As part of this approach, the Village should establish a

clear framework for partnering with private development

to help deliver trail connections, park improvements,

and open space amenities. This may include identifying

priority amenities and connections the Village seeks

from development, such as trail segments, greenway

may consider adjustments to park impact fees or other

while helping the Village expand its park and trail

Implementation will require coordination among Village

connections to Plain City’s natural features, including

waterways and open spaces, helping to preserve and

celebrate the community’s environmental assets.

This network is more than recreation; it is a tool for

shaping growth, encouraging healthy lifestyles, and

connecting people to Plain City’s natural and cultural

assets. Implementing a comprehensive trail and

greenway spine will strengthen the Village’s identity,

support sustainable transportation options, and enhance

community cohesion for generations to come.

RECOMMENDATIONS 107

Provide a strong and safe

multimodal street network

and connect trails

Creating a safe, complete, and connected transportation

network is essential to supporting Plain City’s growth

and everyday quality of life. As the Village continues

to evolve, how people move through and between

neighborhoods, schools, parks, Uptown, and job centers

will shape both accessibility and community character.

Plain City’s strategy is to build a transportation system

that works for everyone—linking local destinations

while connecting to the broader regional trail network so

people of all ages and abilities can travel comfortably by

foot, bike, mobility device, car, and even farm equipment.

Today, many of Plain City’s streets and corridors are

designed primarily for vehicles, and gaps in sidewalks,

crossings, and trails can make it difficult or unsafe for

residents to navigate without a car. As growth continues,

these gaps become more pronounced, particularly

in areas experiencing new development. Improving

connectivity within and between neighborhoods is

critical to ensuring that residents can safely access daily

needs, whether they're walking to a park, biking to

school, or reaching Uptown’s businesses and services.

Addressing these challenges will require a thoughtful

and balanced approach to transportation. Enhancing

sidewalks, filling trail gaps, improving crossings, and

calming busy streets can create a safer and more

comfortable environment for all users. These investments

not only improve safety but also support the vitality

of key destinations like Uptown and the park system,

making them more accessible and inviting. At the same

time, maintaining efficient routes for vehicles, freight,

108 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

The multimodal accommodations and walkable nature

of Uptown expand access between neighborhoods and

destinations, including parks, restaurants, and

regional trails.

and agricultural equipment remains an important part of

Plain City’s transportation network and rural identity.

Fixing critical gaps and strengthening connections is

how Plain City can provide real transportation choices

as the community grows. A more connected system

supports healthier lifestyles, reduces reliance on driving,

and enhances the overall livability of the Village. The

recommendations in this section provide a framework

for building a transportation network that is safe, flexible,

and reflective of Plain City’s unique character and

future needs.

CONNECT OUR COMMUNITY

Conditions & Trends

84%

Of the community

commutes by

personal vehicle

Village-wide walkability is limited by gaps and

inconsistencies in the sidewalk network.  Across Plain

City, 55% of roads lack complete sidewalks. While new

residential areas are built with sidewalks, many arterial

and collector roads throughout Plain City either lack

sidewalks entirely or contain fragmented, disconnected

segments that hinder safe pedestrian access to Uptown

or to other amenities.

Most residents rely on personal vehicles for their daily

commute. 84% of employed residents commute via

personal car or carpool, while an additional 15.5% work

from home. This means that only 1% take other forms

of transportation, reflecting both the lack of alternative

transit options and the slim overlap between those who

live and work in Plain City.

The Village’s trail network is expanding but remains

fragmented.  Plain City has roughly 35 miles of

existing and proposed trails. However, many segments

are disconnected or contained within individual

developments, limiting their functionality as a

cohesive system.

"We need walking

paths or sidewalks

from neighborhoods to

Uptown and surrounding

neighborhoods. All public

roads should have a

walking path to utilize as a

form of travel."

Regional roadway access is a defining mobility asset.

The Village is located near US 33, SR 161, I-270, and

US 42, placing multiple major employment centers

within a 45-minute drive. This connectivity supports

regional access but does not translate to local

multimodal mobility.

Local street connectivity varies across the community.

Newer subdivisions tend to include more connected

street networks, while older areas and major corridors

lack continuous pedestrian infrastructure. This results in

uneven accessibility across the Village.

neighborhoods and everyday destinations. By coordinating transportation investments

with land use and development, the Village can create a more accessible and efficient

network that supports all users, strengthens connections to community destinations, and

expands transportation choices for residents.

110 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Implementation Considerations: Closing the Gap on

the Heritage Trail.

Key Constraints to Acknowledge

• Physical barriers:  Quarry operations, floodplain

conditions, and environmental constraints along the

Big Darby Creek corridor limit where a continuous

trail can be located.

• Fragmented ownership: Much of the land needed

to complete the connection is in private ownership,

requiring coordination, easements, or acquisition.

• Right-of-way gaps: There is no continuous public

corridor today to extend the trail directly into

Uptown.

• Funding limitations:  Trail expansion requires a mix

of local, state, and regional funding, which is often

competitive and phased over time.

Why Private Development Matters

• Key implementation partner: New development

along potential alignments can create opportunities

to dedicate easements, build trail segments, or

reserve corridors as part of site planning.

• Cost sharing: Developers can help deliver segments

of the trail network, reducing the burden on public

funding.

• Timing advantage: As land develops, it becomes

easier to secure connections early, rather than

retrofitting later at higher cost.

• Design integration: T Trails can be more seamlessly

integrated into neighborhoods, open spaces, and

stormwater systems when planned alongside

development.

Step the Village Could Take

• Require or incentivize trail easements and stub

connections in new developments

• Leverage the mapped priority corridors to guide

future trail alignment and implementation

• Coordinate with partners like Metro Parks and

regional agencies on funding and alignment

• Pursue grants and partnerships to close key gaps

over time

• Use the development review process to incrementally

build the network

Little Miami Trail, Source: Tri-State Trails

Miami ville Trailyard, Source: Cincinnati Magazine

LITTLE MIAMI SCENIC TRAIL:

Cincinnati, Ohio

The Little Miami Scenic Trail, located along the

Little Miami River in Southwestern Ohio, is the

longest trail in the Miami Valley Trail network.

A former railway line, this 78.1 mile scenic trail

passes through five counties in Ohio.

Amenities along the trail include free parking lots,

restrooms, picnic tables, parks, and restaurants,

making it a popular destination connecting

multiple cities including Yellow Springs,

Loveland, Milford, Xenia, and Terrace Park. The

trail also connects to the Buckeye Trail.

The rail corridor was funded by $1 million from

the federal Bureau of Outdoor Recreation and

matching state funds. Construction of the first

13-mile section, located between Loveland and

Morrow, was completed in 1984.

Communities along the trail have embraced

its positive impact. The trail has become an

integral part of Loveland's landscape and

city identity, being called "the front door" to

Downtown Loveland and connecting with several

neighborhood amenities.

CONNECT OUR COMMUNITY | RECOMMENDATIONS 111

Existing and Proposed Trails

Plain City's existing trail network is limited, constraining

safe and convenient multimodal travel options. Most

of the Village lacks continuous pedestrian and bicycle

infrastructure, making it difficult to access parks,

neighborhoods, and natural areas safely and comfortably

without a vehicle. This gap in connectivity is particularly

evident in the limited number of off-street paths and

the absence of a direct link to regional trail systems,

restricting opportunities for both daily mobility and

recreational use.

Several proposed bike routes and trails shown on Figure

15 demonstrate the Village's emphasis on improving

connectivity. Planned on-road bike routes, shared use

paths, natural surface trails, and development-driven trail

systems will expand the network across the community.

A key opportunity is the potential extension of the

Heritage Trail into Plain City, which would establish

a critical connection to the broader Central Ohio

greenway system. These planned improvements represent

an important step toward creating a more complete, safe,

and accessible trail network that better supports both

transportation and recreation.

CONNECT OUR COMMUNITY | RECOMMENDATIONS 113

Source: City of Alexandria, VA

SAFE ROUTES TO SCHOOL

Alexandria, Virginia

In Alexandria, Virginia, the city collaborated with

its school district, parent organizations, and local

advocacy groups to implement a comprehensive

Safe Routes to School (SRTS) program that

combined infrastructure improvements with

behavioral initiatives. As part of the effort, a

locally focused SRTS "how-to" manual was

developed, providing teachers and parents with

step-by-step instructions, tips, and strategies to

organize safe walking and biking events at their

schools. The city also created school-travel plans,

upgraded sidewalks and crossings near schools,

and integrated walking and biking routines into

the school culture. These combined efforts led to

better coordination among stakeholders, increased

walking and biking rates, and a sustainable

program that has now become a routine part of

school planning across the city.

114 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

5.2 Safe Routes to School & School-Area

Street Program

Creating safe and accessible routes to school is an

important part of supporting healthy lifestyles and

reducing traffic congestion around school campuses. This

effort should focus on improving sidewalks, crossings,

signage, lighting, and traffic calming measures in

school areas to better accommodate students walking

or biking. Coordination with the school district and

ongoing evaluation of arrival and dismissal patterns

will help identify priority improvements and ensure

that infrastructure investments respond to real safety

concerns. Enhancing these connections will not only

improve student safety but also strengthen neighborhood

walkability and everyday mobility for residents.

5.3 Uptown-Focused Complete Streets Projects

Uptown Plain City serves as the heart of the community,

functioning as a center for local businesses, civic

activity, and daily interaction. As traffic volumes and

development pressures increase, there is a growing need

to ensure that Uptown streets are designed to safely

and comfortably accommodate all users. Implementing

Complete Streets principles within Uptown will help

balance the needs of pedestrians, cyclists, transit users,

and drivers while reinforcing the area’s character and

inherent walkability.

This effort should focus on rethinking street design to

prioritize safety, accessibility, and placemaking. Potential

improvements may include widened sidewalks, enhanced

crosswalks, traffic calming measures, on-street parking

strategies, bicycle accommodations, and streetscape

elements such as lighting, landscaping, and seating. These

enhancements can create a more inviting and human

scaled environment that encourages walking, supports

the local businesses, and improves the overall experience

of Uptown.

Careful consideration will be needed to balance

competing demands within a constrained right-of

way, particularly where through-traffic, parking, and

pedestrian activity intersect. Engaging business owners,

residents, and stakeholders will be critical to identifying

solutions that maintain access while improving safety

and comfort for all users.

5.3 Uptown-Focused Complete Streets Projects

Continued

Develop a policy that outlines a framework for

applying these best practices in project planning, capital

improvements, and development review.

Definition:

"Complete Streets" " are streets designed for all kinds of

road users, aiming to create a safe and convenient journey

regardless of chosen mode, age, or physical condition. Complete

Street can:

• Address chronic disease (e.g., asthma, diabetes, heart

disease)

• Increase physical activity

• Improve safety

• Mitigate human exposure to transportation-related

emissions

• Decrease motor vehicle-related injuries and fatalities

• Reduce transportation’s contribution to air pollution

Harvard Ave, Cleveland, Source: Bike Cleveland

COMPLETE STREETS

Cleveland, OH

Complete Streets began as a grassroots

movement, and is now nationally recognized as a

way to formally codify safer streets for a diverse

array of users.

The City of Cleveland operates a "Complete

and Green Streets" program, with a high degree

of transparency and public-facing interaction.

Following a 2022 Complete Streets ordinance,

a committee meets regularly to discuss potential

projects. Comments are posted publicly explaining

the choice of particular “Complete Streets”

elements. They produce a monthly newsletter

with Complete Streets updates, as well as publish

annual progress reports. Additionally, the city's

multimodal transportation plan is updated every

five years.

Highlights from the 2023 progress report

include metrics on additional trees planted, ADA

curb replacements, sidewalk repairs, enhanced

crosswalks, and community engagement events.

The report highlights progress indicators and

upcoming projects relating to Complete Streets

initiatives around the city.

The concept of a bypass is not new to Plain

City. Previous planning efforts, including the

2009 Thoroughfare Plan, identified the potential

for alternative roadway connections to help

redirect regional traffic around Uptown. This

idea remains relevant today, particularly as

development activity increases across the SR 161

corridor and surrounding areas. Recent traffic

analysis continues to highlight the physical

constraints of widening SR 161 through

Uptown, suggesting that additional connections

or bypass routes may be a more feasible long

term strategy for managing regional traffic and

preserving the function and character of the

Village core.

This study should assess current and projected

traffic patterns, including the volume and type

of vehicles traveling through Uptown, and

widening, roundabout construction, and intersection realignments.

Source: Delaware County Transit

ON-DEMAND TRANSIT

Delaware, OH

Delaware County's FLEX service offers on

demand, curb-to-curb service for those in

Delaware County who don't have a car or prefer

not to travel by car. With service hours from 5 am

to 9 pm, residents can schedule same-day rides for

drop off and pickup. Fares are based on rider age

to ensure equitable access to transportation.

Riders also can choose a "Demand Response" ride,

providing longer but more expensive trips to areas

further away and outside of Delaware County.

Trips must be scheduled at least one business day

in advance.

RTA CONNECT

Dayton, OH

In Dayton, RTA Connect On-Demand operates

curb-to-curb service in areas with low fixed-route

service. On-demand service is $4 per ride, and the

agency partners with Lyft to ensure efficient pick

ups and drop-offs.

These case studies present innovative alternatives

to public transportation in areas that are too small,

too far from a major city, or have too little demand

to justify a more robust transit system. While Plain

City may be integrated into the COTA service

network eventually, an on-demand service could be

a more feasible short-term solution.

118 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

5.5 Explore Regional Transit Opportunities

Evaluate the need for potential public transit service

through various regional providers.

As Plain City continues to grow, exploring regional

transit options can help expand mobility choices and

improve access to jobs, services, and surrounding

communities. This effort should assess potential

partnerships with regional transit providers, including

connections to Central Ohio systems such as Central

Ohio Transit Authority (COTA), as well as nearby

communities like Dublin, Marysville, and the broader

Columbus region. Opportunities may include commuter

routes, on-demand transit services, or park-and-ride

facilities that link residents to major employment centers.

While transit may not be an immediate need, early

coordination and ongoing dialogue with regional

agencies will position the Village to take advantage of

future service expansions. By proactively planning for

regional connectivity, including preparing for a potential

Amtrak rail station proposed a few miles from Plain

City, the Village can improve access, support workforce

mobility, and ensure residents remain connected to

the broader Central Ohio transportation network and

beyond as the community evolves.

Plain City’s proximity to the COTA network is an

important asset as the Village continues to grow.

Located just 9.4 miles from the nearest park-and-ride,

options, especially valuable given that most residents

work outside the Village. Today, limited transit access

means heavy reliance on private vehicles, but planned

investments like the LinkUS Northwest Corridor will

expand transit through nearby Dublin, strengthening

regional connections. As population growth continues,

Plain City is well-positioned for potential future service

expansion, offering more commuting options and

improved regional accessibility beyond.

THOROUGHFARE

TYPOLOGIES

The thoroughfare typologies developed for Plain City are

reflective of three distinct character classifications seen

throughout the Village and surrounding areas: Urban,

Suburban, and  Rural. These character classifications

form concentric “rings” around Plain City, beginning

with urban character in the Village center and radiating

outward to the rural edges of the community. Within

each of these character classifications there are three

roadway typologies, forming a total of nine roadway

typologies. Each typology illustrates aspirational

conditions for roadways within Plain City that are

reflective of the Village’s historic small-town character.

While each road’s functional classification is taken into

consideration, these roadway typologies provide more

detail about the look and feel of roads in each transect of

the Village while considering their traffic volumes and

functional needs.

URBAN TYPOLOGY:

UPTOWN SIGNATURE

Signature roads within the Uptown district where most activity is concentrated, these

roads exhibit a strong sense of place and identity. They support vehicular traffic flow and

provide a comfortable and inviting pedestrian experience.

The Uptown Signature typology supports the Village's

lively Uptown district, introducing elements that reflect

Plain City's identity and elevate the area's appearance.

These streets should be well-lit, multimodal, and lush

with street trees and native landscaping. Well-designed

streets within Uptown help maintain the Village's small

town charm, establishing a quaint and welcoming feel for

visitors to shop, dine, and gather in the district.

The Uptown Signature typology is applied to segments

of Main Street, Chillicothe Street, and Gay Street in

the heart of Uptown. The intersection of these three

streets are the focal point of the Village, used daily for

travel, activity, and year-round community events. Local Main Street, Source: Google Earth

restaurants, shops, gathering places, and nearby parks

generate significant activity within this core. At the

same time, Main Street and Chillicothe Street are key

community thoroughfares that continue beyond the

community and link to neighboring destinations like

Columbus, Dublin, West Jefferson, and Hilliard.

The Uptown Signature street segments today are

functional, however, small improvements can refresh

safety and aesthetics. Existing enhancements, like

streetscape plantings and decorative banners, form

the foundation for an inviting experience for Uptown

patrons. Maintaining the vehicular flow of these roads

and incorporating more pedestrian-focused elements is

vital to creating a pleasant experience for all visitors to

the Village. Chillicothe Street, Source: Google Earth

The Uptown Signature road supports all modes of

transportation with capacity for high amounts of foot

traffic to encourage an energetic, inviting Uptown

experience for visitors. The historic development pattern

of Uptown leaves little room for expansion of the current

streetscape, but small enhancements like the addition of

more plantings, street furniture, and striped crosswalks

can help elevate the appearance of Uptown Signature

streets and support comfort and safety for all road users.

Single drive lanes in each direction with turn lanes

at intersections keeps traffic flowing smoothly, but

discourages speeding through a pedestrian-focused

environment. Striped on-street parking is incorporated

on both sides of the road to maintain parking options

that residents and visitors value. To maximize pedestrian

comfort and safety, striped crosswalks with sidewalk

bump-outs are provided at intersections. These elements

help amplify visibility for pedestrians and drivers. Trees,

landscaping, light poles, wayfinding signage, and street

furniture all support pedestrian comfort and access while

enhancing the appearance and identity of Uptown.

The Uptown Signature road typology represents some

of Plain City's most iconic and memorable streets. These

streets are often the setting for community events like

parades, markets, and festivals, making identity and

community pride a central component of the proposed

Uptown Signature character. Seasonal decorations

including banners, flowers, and holiday lights can be

easily incorporated into the streetscape year-round.

Wayfinding signage in and around Uptown along major

routes can help visitors navigate the district and direct

more foot traffic to businesses. Building a cohesive,

consistent identity throughout the public realm ensures

Uptown remains a hub of pride for the community.

To further support vibrancy and activity in Uptown,

businesses within the district are encouraged to extend

their activity onto the street. Outdoor dining, sidewalk

sales, and window displays all create a more enticing

Uptown experience, inviting visitors to spend more time

in the district and contributing to a healthier, livelier

community core.

Balancing pedestrian experiences with vehicular access is

critical for Uptown Signature Streets. With Main Street

and Chillicothe Street being highly-traveled arterial

roads that support truck and vehicular traffic, streets

need to be adequately designed not only to ensure traffic

remains smooth and delays are minimized, but to avoid

disrupting the overall Uptown experience. Maintaining

turn lanes at intersections and signalized pedestrian

crossings can help facilitate vehicular movement and

reduce points of conflict within Uptown.

URBAN TYPOLOGY:

TRADITIONAL RESIDENTIAL

Historic Village streets that support compact development patterns and a mix of

residential and neighborhood-scale commercial uses. Buildings are typically oriented

close to the street, reinforcing a walkable environment within and near the Village core.

Traditional Residential streets support a dense mix

of homes, small businesses, and civic uses while

accommodating on-street parking and pedestrian

movement. These streets honor the historic

neighborhood development pattern surrounding

Uptown. Garages and parking areas are typically

accessed through rear alleys, allowing for a more timeless

neighborhood appearance along primary roads.

Existing Traditional Residential streets vary widely in

design. The two streets pictured on this page represent

the broad spectrum of existing conditions found on

residential streets near Uptown. Gay Street features

recent improvements that incorporate striped onGay Street, Source: Google Earth

street parking, planted bump-outs, new sidewalks, and

a curb-and-gutter system to collect stormwater. The

street was converted to one-way traffic as part of these

improvements. By contrast, Shepper Avenue is a curbless

street that allows two-way traffic with unmarked on

street parking and no sidewalks.

More cohesion among the various Traditional

Residential streets in the Village core can reinforce

identity and community, promoting walkability and a

more pleasant experience for residents and businesses.

These streets should maintain on-street parking access,

but should also provide pedestrians. While space is

limited due to buildings constructed close to roads,

targeted enhancements similar to those seen on Gay

Street can create a lasting impact on the neighborhood.Shepper Avenue, Source: Google Earth

Vision for Traditional Residential Streets

4

2 1

Right-of-way: 45–60'

Traditional Residential streets should be designed to

prioritize access to businesses and residences and support

pedestrian movement to and from Uptown. Sidewalks

may either be installed directly adjacent to the curb or

with a landscaped tree lawn separating the sidewalk from

the street, depending on right-of-way availability and

other spatial constraints. Parallel parking should remain

on one side of the street; narrower road widths do not

allow for bi-directional street parking to fit comfortably

within the existing right-of-way.

Access to garages, driveways, or parking lots should be

directed to alleys to preserve the communal, pedestrian

oriented feel of Traditional Residential streets. This

reinforces the historic character by keeping the

pedestrian-oriented environment and maximizes parking

availability on primary streets.

3

5

1. 10-11' drive lanes in each direction with on-street

3. Decorative light poles and street trees

4. Rear access garages and parking areas

5. Small setbacks from right-of-way

Well-appointed Traditional Residential streets evoke

the small town charm that so many Plain City residents

want to see preserved as the Village grows. Designing

streets to protect this historic fabric helps maintain

the timeless appeal of Uptown and its surrounding

neighborhoods. These streets should complement the

Uptown Signature typology, utilizing similar design

elements like plantings and light poles to extend the

Uptown identity to residential areas.

Traditional Residential streets should support walkability

by providing a continuous sidewalk network that

connects residents to Uptown and other surrounding

destinations like parks, trails, and other commercial

nodes throughout the Village. These streets should also

incorporate crosswalks at intersections and utilize bump

outs in appropriate locations similar to the project on

Gay Street. Bump-outs can be used not only for greater

pedestrian visibility at crosswalks, but also for additional

greenery and plantings.

Fostering more pedestrian connectivity and enhancing

the public realm in residential areas can encourage

more walking, generate more economic activity, increase

property values and elevate overall neighborhood appeal.

URBAN TYPOLOGY:

TRADITIONAL ALLEY

Narrow through roads that provide rear access to homes and businesses, allowing parking

and loading activity to occur away from primary thoroughfares.

Vehicular access throughout the historic Village core is

aided by a network of alleys that allow circulation for

rear-facing driveways, garages, parking lots and pads,

or deliveries. These alleys generally do not support

continuous two-way traffic, but most currently allow for

bi-directional access due to their low traffic volumes.

While these alleys may not be frequented by visitors,

they are a key component of the Uptown identity, and

should be designed to support local circulation.

Some residential streets may be classified as alleys,

even if they do not have "alley" in their names, due

to the narrow width and overall function of the road.

Designating these roads as alleys alleviates the need Center Street, Source: Google Earth

for them to meet the conditions desired for typical

residential streets, as many may not have the necessary

right-of-way to incorporate desired pedestrian

accommodations or on-street parking spaces.

Vision for Traditional Alley Streets

4

2

Traditional Alley streets are used primarily by residents

to access garages and driveways located behind homes.

Alleys may be designated as one-way roads, or they may

allow for bi-directional traffic by allowing one car to

travel through at a time.

Due to their very low traffic, pedestrians and cyclists may

choose to share the road with vehicles. To help reinforce

comfort and safety, street lights that match the Uptown

identity should be installed to increase visibility.

Traditional Alley segments that provide access to

Uptown businesses may incorporate additional elements

to support higher pedestrian and vehicular traffic and

promote increased safety. Installing sidewalks in alleys

between buildings and drive lanes can allow designated

space for pedestrian movement to and from businesses.

Safety bollards can provide physical buffers between

roadways and pedestrian pathways. Deliveries to

Uptown businesses should be directed to alleys to avoid

congestion on primary roads.
