Alleys in Plain City are a unique remnant of the

Village's past, and should be maintained and uplifted

as functional access roads for residents in the historic

Village core. While some streetscape elements like street

trees and sidewalks might not be feasible, alleys can still

play a valuable role in defining Plain City's identity.

Ensuring the Village's alleys remain clean, well-kept,

and accessible to residents helps keep other roads in the

urban core functioning properly.

Although they are generally located out of the public eye

compared to other roads, Traditional Alleys can still be

designed to amplify the overall character of the Village.

For example, adding lighting and maintaining curbless

conditions with grass edges reinforces the Village

character with small but impactful improvements. More

transformational improvements, like installing a "grassed

lane" similar to the example pictured to the right, would

require additional buy-in from the Village and residents

but could present a more ecological, countryside

character to complement Plain City's rural context.

Traditional Alley segments closer to Uptown exist in

a more commercial setting and have slightly different

functions. These alleys should still be well-maintained

but designed for delivery vehicles to access back-of

house and loading areas for businesses. Additionally,

some alleys provide access to parking lots located behind

businesses for Uptown workers and visitors. These roads

may feature curbs and gutters due to the higher levels

of impervious surfaces in the areas. Lighting and access

management may be more critical in these alleys to

ensure efficient and clear traffic flow from parking lots

and loading zones.

SUBURBAN TYPOLOGY:

COMMUNITY COLLECTOR

Streets that link residential neighborhoods to community destinations such as parks,

schools, and civic buildings. Comfortable pedestrian pathways, bike facilities, and traffic

calming measures allow all community members to utilize these roads safely.

Community Collector streets support traffic from

various neighborhoods while providing safe pedestrian

and bicycle routes and supporting access to homes

and businesses. Uses along these streets typically

include homes, civic centers, and neighborhood

serving businesses. These roads also connect to various

neighborhoods throughout Plain City and provide access

to regional transportation networks. Their role as a link

between neighborhoods and destinations means they

need to facilitate moderate amounts of vehicular traffic

and provide pedestrian facilities near community assets.

Community Collector segments typically lead to

community facilities like parks, schools, and the Plain Chillicothe Street near Pastime Park, Source: Google Earth

City Municipal Building, though they also connect to

neighborhood commercial areas. Multimodal traffic is

supported by sidewalks and "sharrows" designating lanes

to be shared by vehicles and cyclists.

Community Collector streets should be accessible

for all modes of transportation while maintaining a

neighborhood feel for residents and businesses. By

providing street trees, separate bicycle facilities, and

right-sized roads, the Community Collector typology

can provide a neighborhood roadway for all users that

minimizes dangerous driving and provides comfortable

options for active transportation.

Vision for Community Collector Streets

Community Collector streets facilitate moderate

amounts of vehicular traffic and provide separate

pedestrian and cyclist infrastructure to maximize safe

connections throughout the Village. These streets

generally feature a single drive lane in each direction,

but provide separate turn lanes at major signalized

intersections to prevent congestion.

To provide safe connections to community destinations,

shared use paths are provided along Community

Connector streets. These paths connect to various

neighborhoods and can link residents to local and

regional recreational facilities like Pastime Park and the

Heritage Trail. Lighting and street trees further enhance

the streetscape, transforming collector roads from

primarily vehicular routes to essential pathways that

encourage active transportation.

Community Collector streets feed into many of

Plain City's neighborhoods and connect residents to

community parks, schools, commercial districts, and

commuter routes. They are able to handle higher traffic

volumes comfortably without increasing risks for

pedestrians and cyclists.

As many community members interact with Community

Collector streets on a daily basis, beautification of these

streets can reinforce civic pride and improve experiences

for roadway users. Lighting for both roads and shared

uniform appearance along the street. Near major

destinations like parks and schools, streetscape elements

such as trash cans, benches, planter boxes, and mid-block

crosswalks provide added safety and comfort.

Some existing Community Collector streets within

subdivision developments have additional features that

further the community-serving functionality of this

road typology. Landscaped medians on Darby Station

Boulevard serve as a neighborhood gateway element,

and striped on-street parking on Madison Way provides

vehicular access to Madison Meadows Park. These

elements, while not depicted in the typology diagram,

are welcome variations that help Community Collector

streets better serve residents.

SUBURBAN TYPOLOGY:

BUSINESS PARK TRANSITIONAL

Connector streets that link major commuter routes and residential neighborhoods to

commercial and industrial business districts outside of the Village core.

With the potential for a new mixed-use district in the

Creekview Commerce Park, Business Park Transitional

streets can help ease the transition from adjacent

residential areas into a commercial setting that integrates

with surrounding neighborhoods. This roadway typology

does not exist in Plain City today, but multiple existing

segments and proposed future roads could embody this

type of road in the Village.

The existing segments designated as Business Park

Transitional segments are along Butler Avenue and

Converse Road in the northeast quadrant of Plain City.

These roads today are very rural in character, with narrow

lanes and low traffic volumes. Ensuring these roads, and Converse Road, Source: Google Earth

any new roads, can accommodate commuter traffic to the

business district will minimize disruptions and ensure

successful community growth.

Business Park Transitional roads should be calm yet

efficient, with minimal curb cuts and consolidated

access points to simplify traffic flow. These roads should

feature plenty of trees, native plantings, and green

spaces to maintain a balance between development and

naturalized areas. The goal of this road typology is to

ease the transition from residential areas to employment

centers and create a pleasant atmosphere for all users.

Vision for Business Park Transitional Streets

31

Business Park Transitional streets allow for smooth

vehicular traffic flow paired with pedestrian and bicycle

infrastructure to connect people to jobs. These streets

should be designed to accommodate higher volumes

of traffic during rush hour, connect to major commuter

routes, and link access to other regional employment

nodes. Multimodal access and pedestrian comfort can

encourage healthier habits for street users by promoting

alternative commuting methods and providing

comfortable spaces for pedestrian use.

Business Park Transitional streets support commuter

traffic with wider right-of-way allowances and dedicated

turn lanes, using planted medians and tree-lined streets

to encourage safe driving and maintain a picturesque,

green appearance that embodies the established character

of Plain City. Parking areas are located behind buildings

to reduce visual clutter and maximize green buffers along

the road.

The Business Park Transitional road typology brings

together a holistic design approach that integrates

mixed-use development and job opportunities with

complete streets and active transportation options.

Streets are designed to be multimodal and green,

forming a healthy, thriving community spine where

economic opportunity and transportation connections

are abundant.

Shared use paths connect residents to jobs and

encourage alternative modes of transportation. In

addition to providing options for commuters, these

paths also provide recreational assets for workers to

use during business hours. These elements, together

with landscaping and lighting, make Business Park

Transitional streets inviting and unique, creating a new

road typology in the Village that supports future growth

and honors Plain City's identity.

craft a unique identity for the district. Native plantings

and street trees help amplify this identity, balancing

development and activity with abundant greenery.

SUBURBAN TYPOLOGY:

SUBURBAN RESIDENTIAL

Neighborhood streets serving lower density subdivision-style development with primarily

residential uses and a timeless community character.

Suburban Residential streets are distinct from the

Traditional Residential typology seen in the Village

center due to larger home setbacks, wider rights-of-way,

and more consistent, uniform standards for roadway

designs and development patterns. They are calm,

quiet streets that provide local access to homes while

accommodating on-street parking and sidewalks for

neighborhood pedestrian activity. Homes are typically

set back farther from the road with front driveway

access. Pedestrian pathways connect households

to neighborhood parks and schools, embodying a

communal character that supports the tight-knit feel of

these neighborhoods.

Alderwood Circle, Source: Google Earth

Many of Plain City's roads fall under the Suburban

Residential typology. These road designs are the most

consistent and uniform across the Village, but it is

critical for Plain City to continue enforcing high-quality

road design standards in all existing and new residential

neighborhoods to ensure consistent end products that

meet all users' needs.

Willow Creek Drive, Source: Google Earth

Vision for Suburban Residential Streets

3

4

1

Suburban Residential streets are designed for local

residential traffic and neighborhood use. Their

appearance embodies a quintessential suburban setting

with consistent setbacks, continuous sidewalks, and tree

lawns to separate pedestrians from the roadway.

Traffic is calm enough that cyclists can share the road

with vehicles, but some neighborhoods may choose to

incorporate shared use paths to encourage to a more

active, multimodal community. Street lights and trees

planted in tree lawns further separate pedestrians from

the roadway and help beautify the neighborhood. On

street parking is generally permitted on both sides of

the street, and moving vehicles are expected to yield to

oncoming traffic before passing parked cars.

144 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

2

Right-of-way: 60'

1. One 11'-12' drive lane in each direction with on

street parking permitted on both sides

2. 5' sidewalks on each side (can use 10' shared use

paths where appropriate) with tree lawn

3. Lighting and street trees

4. Front access driveways and moderate setbacks

Suburban Residential streets should provide crosswalks

at intersections and connections to recreational assets

like parks and trails whenever possible. Intersections are

encouraged to use all-way stop signs to encourage safe

driving and limit speeding in residential areas. Areas

with high pedestrian traffic, such as schools or parks, are

encouraged to use high-visibility crosswalk striping and

pedestrian-activated flashing signals to facilitate safer

crossings at key destinations.

The design of Suburban Residential streets should

match the scale of development in the neighborhood.

Larger homes, deeper setbacks, and longer driveways

allow roads to incorporate larger tree lawns and taller

trees to appropriately reflect the neighborhood's scale.

Decorative light poles with underground electrical

conduit contribute to a more traditional neighborhood

aesthetic and reduces visual clutter.

Suburban Residential streets incorporate stormwater

management practices, typically using curb-and-gutter

systems and on-site retention ponds to manage runoff.

Alternative approaches, such as vegetated swales or

curbless roads, can promote healthier water quality. These

design choices should be evaluated by the Village on a

case-by-case basis to ensure road designs are consistent

with the desired community character.

RURAL TYPOLOGY:

GATEWAY ARTERIAL

Major entry corridors that create a recognizable and welcoming arrival experience into

Plain City. These routes balance high traffic volumes with enhanced landscaping, signage,

and design elements that reinforce community identity.

Gateway Arterial streets mark the approach to Plain

City, supporting traffic flow while creating visual

indicators that introduce the Village to visitors and

commuters entering via primary corridors. This typology

is concentrated on segments that support the most

traffic, particularly where commuters travel to other

major employment centers in the region. These roads

balance traffic management with community identity to

create a gateway experience that is distinctly Plain City.

Gateway Arterials in the Village include SR 161 east of

Butler Avenue and Plain City-Georgesville Road south

of Darby Fields Boulevard. These two segments mark

critical entryways to the Village, connecting to larger SR 161, Source: Google Earth

communities like Columbus, Dublin, and Hilliard. Many

commuters and visitors use these routes to travel to and

from Plain City.

The existing character of these roads is distinctly rural,

with single drive lanes in each direction and naturalized

edges. Pedestrian pathways have been installed far

from the roadway in limited areas as part of suburban

development. As primary gateways to the community,

Gateway Arterial roads have the opportunity to create

a more distinct, welcoming approach to Plain City

that honors the Village's legacy and personality while

enhancing driver and pedestrian experiences.

NE Plain City-Georgesville Road, Source: Google Earth

Gateway Arterial streets are intended to conduct higher

traffic volumes and announce entry points into Plain

City. On major arterial roads (i.e. SR 161), up to two

lanes in each direction may be utilized, with a center

median allowing for dedicated left turn lanes. Signature

landscaping, abundant trees, and welcome signage

create a memorable entry experience for residents and

visitors, broadcasting the Village's identity and pride.

Wayfinding signage can guide visitors to key destinations

like Uptown, Pastime Park, or other local business and

shopping districts.

At high-traffic intersections, roundabouts may be used

to facilitate traffic flow. Roundabouts should also feature

signature landscaping and adequate signage to guide

traffic efficiently.

Shared use paths that connect to the broader regional

trail network further establish Gateway Arterial streets

as primary entryways to the Village. Shared use paths

should be separated from the road with street trees and

wide landscaped buffers.

high-visibility crosswalks with flashing lights should

be installed where pathways connect across streets to

recreational areas.

RURAL TYPOLOGY:

RURAL COMMUTER

Major corridors that connect Plain City to surrounding communities and the regional

road network. These routes are primarily used for commuting, freight movement, and

longer-distance travel while maintaining a rural roadway character.

Rural Commuter corridors allow efficient and

continuous traffic flow with access to neighborhoods

concentrated at few intersections to reduce congestion

caused by turning vehicles. These roads are some of the

most heavily used routes in the Village. They feature

larger setbacks from the roadway and wide shoulders

to allow farm equipment to travel safely next to higher

speed vehicles.

Rural Commuter road segments in Plain City include

all of US 42 and parts of SR 161 west of US 42. These

routes extend far beyond the Village, connecting

Plain City to the broader Central Ohio region. These

routes have limited pedestrian facilities that are often US 42/Jefferson Avenue, Source: Google Earth

unprotected, with sidewalks built directly next to

vehicular lanes. Restaurants, retail centers, and civic

buildings are located along these roads, with some

residential homes built along SR 161.

As major arterial roads through the Village, Rural

Commuter roads prioritize efficient and safe traffic flow.

However, with several popular community destinations

and retail centers along these roads, providing

accommodations for pedestrians and cyclists to access

businesses and civic buildings can transform these roads

into more complete streets that accommodate all modes

of travel.

SR 161 west of US 42, Source: Google Earth

Vision for Rural Commuter Streets

3

2

4 5

Right-of-way: 80–100'

Rural Commuter streets serve as major arterial roads

that connect Plain City to regional employment

destinations and commuter routes. These roads are

designed to support a wide range of transportation types,

from pedestrians and cyclists to freight carriers. Wide

shoulders allow for farm equipment to safely use these

streets without causing disruptions for other vehicles.

The ample right-of-way available on Rural Commuter

streets allows for shared use paths that provide dedicated

space for pedestrians and cyclists. These paths are

separated by landscaped buffers to enhance aesthetics

and increase perceptions of pedestrian and cyclist safety

along busy, high-speed roads. With businesses and civic

centers located along Rural Commuter routes, providing

safe connections for all road users allows community

members greater freedom to choose the mode of

transportation that works best for their individual needs.

152 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

1

6

1. 11'-12' drive lane in each direction

2. Center 11' two-way left turn lane

3. Wide shoulders on both sides to allow for farm

equipment travel

4. 10' shared use path on both sides of road

5. 6' minimum landscaped buffer between road and

shared use path

While Rural Commuter streets are primarily designed

for utility, enhancing their appearance within Plain

City's boundaries helps create a sense of place as users

pass through the Village. Street trees and lighting for

roads and shared use paths should be incorporated

wherever feasible, prioritizing areas near businesses and

activity centers.

Clear signage, wayfinding, and access can help minimize

visual clutter and bring more organization to Rural

Commuter roadways. Village entry signage should be

used at Plain City's boundaries along these roads and

should be designed to match the signage used along

Gateway Arterial streets. Signage for local businesses

should be highly visible and located close to the right

of-way. Signage directing visitors to Uptown and

other community destinations should be provided at a

scale visible to both drivers and shared use path users.

Access drives to businesses and retail centers should

be consolidated to minimize curb cuts and keep traffic

flowing smoothly, using entryways on side streets where

possible to consolidate traffic at signalized intersections.

Reducing conflict points along Rural Commuter roads

prevents traffic congestion and reduces risks for drivers

and pedestrians alike. Fewer curb cuts and consolidated

entries allow for more continuous shared use paths and

more opportunities to beautify the streetscape.

RURAL TYPOLOGY:

RURAL RESIDENTIAL

Low-traffic roads serving very low density housing, farmland, and rural properties. These

roads provide access to rural homes, farms, and agricultural operations.

Rural Residential roads are located at the edges of the

community extending outside Village limits and connect

rural areas to the broader road network. They support low

traffic volumes and sometimes permit higher speeds due

to their low-density development patterns and straight

geometry. Properties along these roads often feature

large setbacks, long entry drives, and very low density,

reinforcing a natural, agricultural environment.

Existing Rural Residential roads in and around the

Village include Cemetery Pike, Converse Huff Road,

and Lafayette-Plain City Road. These roads connect

to major arterial roads in the area as well as various

suburban neighborhoods in Plain City, tying the Converse Huff Road, Source: Google Earth

Village's road network to other regional locations via

low-traffic country roads.

Delivery trucks, farm equipment, and personal vehicles

use Rural Residential roads to access homes, businesses,

and farms in the outskirts of the Village. On Cemetery

Pike, bike route signs direct cyclists to the access point

for the Heritage Trail. Any pedestrians and cyclists who

wish to use these routes to access regional trails, or who

simply want to walk or bike along the road, must share

the right-of-way with motorized vehicles.

Lafayette-Plain City Road, Source: Google Earth

Vision for Rural Residential Streets

Right-of-way: 60'

Rural Residential streets typically feature higher speeds

and low-to-moderate traffic volumes. These streets

maintain a rural, spacious character with large properties

and infrequent access drives. Parking is not permitted on

Rural Residential streets, but slightly wider lanes allow

for farm equipment transportation with room for other

vehicles to pass.

The primary change proposed to Rural Residential

streets is the addition of shared use paths to connect

residents to recreational assets like parks and regional

trails. Grass buffers and street trees provide additional

safety for path users. Providing shared use paths along

Rural Residential streets can broaden the local bicycle

network and expand access to regional trail systems.

Separate bike facilities along these roads increase cyclists'

perception of safety while maintaining the natural

appearance of Plain City's rural perimeter.

156 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

3

4

1. 11'-12' drive lane in each direction

2. Curbless road with grass swales for stormwater

management

3. 10' shared use path on one side of road

4. 4-6' landscaped buffer with street trees between road

and shared use path

Rural Residential roads are meant to exhibit the idyllic

scenery of the countryside surrounding Plain City.

Unlike the urban and suburban residential typologies,

where road striping isn't necessary, Rural Residential

roads should include painted striping that indicates

where vehicles are allowed to pass.

For pedestrians and cyclists who want to use these

roads, a shared use path allows a safer, more inviting

option for non-motorized travel. These paths can create

a highly-valued recreational asset for the community,

allowing residents and visitors to actively explore the

scenic outskirts of Plain City on a dedicated trail,

potentially connecting to other regional trails and nearby

recreational facilities like the Heritage Trail or various

Village parks. High-visibility crosswalks near recreational

facilities can help ease access to parks and trails.

As growth within the Village approaches these rural

edges, traffic management may become a greater concern

for residents and workers who use these roads. To

preserve the rural character and prevent congestion on

Rural Residential roads, traffic should be directed to use

primary arterials roads whenever possible. The Village

may explore strategic locations to install roundabouts at

Village gateways or entries to suburban neighborhoods

where traffic delays are likely. Using roundabouts in lieu

of stop signs or traffic lights can keep traffic flowing

smoothly and reduce overall congestion without the need

to add additional lanes to Rural Residential roads.

CONNECT OUR COMMUNITY | RECOMMENDATIONS 157

Center Uptown as a

walkable, mixed-use

neighborhood for all

residents

Uptown Plain City is the historic and symbolic heart

of the community, and continued investment here is

essential to reinforcing the Village’s identity as it evolves.

With recent planning efforts and public improvements

already underway, there is strong momentum to

build upon. Plain City’s strategy is to make Uptown

the everyday center of community life, adding more

people, activity, and businesses while enhancing streets,

sidewalks, and public spaces to support walking,

gathering, and events.

Uptown plays a unique role in shaping how residents and

visitors experience Plain City. It is where the Village’s

history, small-town character, and local businesses come

together. Recent streetscape improvements and planning

initiatives have set the stage for continued revitalization,

but there is still opportunity to expand its impact.

Increasing residential presence, supporting a mix of local

businesses, and creating more inviting public spaces and

programming can help ensure Uptown remains active

throughout the day and across seasons.

158 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

The Grainery, opened in 2019, is a well-loved Uptown

restaurant and community gathering place—bringing

people together to meet friends, enjoy a meal after local

events, or experience live music.

Investing in Uptown allows Plain City to capture more

local spending, support small businesses, strengthen

property values, and reinforce the community’s identity

in a visible and meaningful way. By building on recent

momentum, the Village can create a vibrant, welcoming

district that reflects its character while adapting to future

needs. The recommendations in this section provide a

framework for projects, programs, and design strategies

that leverage Uptown's existing assets and strengthen its

potential.

BUILD ON MOMENTUM IN UPTOWN

Conditions & Trends

60%

of the community is

within a 15-minute

walk to Uptown

Uptown is centrally located and accessible to a large

share of residents. Nearly 60% of the population lives

within a 15-minute walk. This proximity positions

Uptown as a primary gathering place and focal point of

daily activity.

The Uptown business base is small but functionally

diverse. Th The district includes 24 businesses spanning

dining, retail, personal services, and event spaces. While

varied, the limited scale constrains the overall level

of activity.

Recent investments have enhanced the physical

environment in Uptown. Streetscape improvements,

public spaces, and building reinvestment have

contributed to a more cohesive and active district. These

changes have strengthened its role within the Village.

"Locally owned restaurants,

live entertainment venues,

Village summer movies,

and events like the

Christmas Celebration

keep residents engaged. We

love Plain

City’s Uptown."

Activity levels in Uptown are concentrated around

specific times and uses. W While events and dining

generate foot traffic, activity outside peak periods is more

limited. This reflects the relatively small scale of the

commercial base.

Uptown continues to function as a primary destination

as the Village’s cultural and historic center. Its mix

of uses and central location make it a focal point for

residents, visitors, and local events. However, its size

limits its ability to serve all daily needs locally.

RECOMMENDATIONS

BUILD ON MOMENTUM IN UPTOWN

6.1 A Additional Community Space

Expanding public gathering spaces in Uptown or the

surrounding area will increase the social and civic

identity that already exists today. Recent investments,

including the Depot at Public Square completed in May

2025, along with planned additions such as The Park at

Public Square and The Marketplace at Public Square, are

creating a vibrant hub where residents and visitors of all

ages can gather.

These spaces, along with additional pocket parks, plazas,

and multi-use indoor facilities, can host events, markets,

performances, and community programs, providing

flexible environments that support a wide range of

activities. Looking ahead, the Village will also need to

consider larger or more adaptable gathering spaces that

can accommodate expanded programming, seasonal

events, and increased attendance over time.

By integrating these spaces into streetscapes, parks,

and public buildings, Uptown can foster stronger social

connections and draw residents of all ages.

160 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

The grand opening at the Depot at Public Square

marked the beginning of community gathering. The

planned expansion will provide the opportunity for

farmers markets, youth programs, business gatherings,

and seasonal celebrations, supporting a variety of ways

for residents to connect and engage.

6.2 Strategic Sites & Edges: Concept Plans for Key

Growth Areas

The intersection of Main Street and Chillicothe Street is

one of the most prominent and highly traveled locations

in Uptown, serving as both a gateway and a central point

of daily activity. Given its visibility and importance, it

has the potential to become a defining place—one that

reinforces Plain City’s identity while setting the tone for

future investment and redevelopment.

The Village has already initiated planning and design

efforts focused on this area, including streetscape

enhancements, intersection and safety improvements,

and opportunities for placemaking and public space.

Building on this momentum, Plain City should advance

this intersection as a flagship redevelopment and

placemaking project, establishing a coordinated vision

that integrates building form, site design, roadway

improvements, and the public realm.

Enhancements to sidewalks, crossings, and streetscape

elements can improve safety and walkability, while the

introduction of public gathering spaces or small park

elements will increase patronage of Uptown throughout

the day and across seasons. The project should support a

mix of uses, including ground-floor commercial, upper

story residential or office space, and flexible spaces that

contribute to community life. High-quality design

will be essential, with an emphasis on strong building

frontages, inviting storefronts, shaded pedestrian areas,

and clear connections to surrounding blocks, parking,

and future trail networks.

By leading with a clear vision and implementing visible

improvements, Plain City can use this project to set

expectations for the quality, character, and level of

investment it expects from the development community.

Advancing this effort may include leveraging public

private partnerships, site coordination or assembly,

phased implementation, and targeted infrastructure

investments that align with broader Uptown

improvements.

Over time, this intersection can evolve into a

recognizable center of activity, supporting local

businesses, enhancing safety and accessibility, and

strengthening Uptown as the everyday heart of the

community. Moving this project forward will not only

transform a key site but also build momentum for

continued reinvestment across Uptown.

Civic Park, Source: MKSK

CIVIC PARK

Lawrenceburg, IN

Lawrenceburg Civic Park demonstrates how

a flagship project can anchor downtown

revitalization and guide future private investment.

Developed through a community-led process, the

project replaced underutilized parking lots with

a flexible civic space that supports both daily use

and community events. Key amenities include a

performance stage for concerts and festivals, open

lawn space, a splash pad, playscape, food truck

areas, and spaces for outdoor dining, supporting

activities throughout the day and year.

The park serves as the focal point of a broader

entertainment district, hosting events like summer

festivals, movies, and fitness programming that

draw residents and visitors. As both a daily

community amenity and a regional destination,

Lawrenceburg's Civic Park fills a critical gap

in public open space, strengthens downtown

vibrancy, and establishes a framework for

sustained, place-based economic development.

BUILD ON MOMENTUM IN UPTOWN | RECOMMENDATIONS 161

6.3 Uptown Living Initiative

Attract businesses and complementary housing that

generate steady, all-day activity and strengthen Uptown

as a vibrant community hub.

A thriving Uptown depends on a balanced mix of daily

activities that draw residents, workers, entrepreneurs,

and visitors throughout the day and into the evening.

The Uptown Living Initiative seeks to expand the range

of housing options and commercial opportunities in the

Uptown area, creating a dynamic environment where

people can live, work, shop, and socialize. By attracting

businesses that provide essential services and amenities,

such as cafes, retail stores, personal services, and creative

workspaces, Uptown can become a destination that

Downtown Hudson, OHsupports sustained activity all day long.

fosters a built-in customer base for local businesses and

6.4 Uptown Public Space & Events Spine

Create a connected network of public parks, streets,

and venues that support more events and activities

throughout Uptown.

A public space network is essential to activating Uptown

as a year‑round destination and the heart of Plain City.

The Uptown Public Space & Events Spine envisions a

coordinated series of parks, plazas, streets, and gathering

venues that not only serve recreational needs but also

provide flexible space for community events, markets,

performances, and civic gatherings.

This network builds on existing and proposed assets

including the projects at Public Square and other

planned improvements, while emphasizing connectivity

to unite these spaces as one cohesive system. The spine

should weave together spaces for everyday use (such

as small plazas and pocket parks) with larger venues

capable of hosting festivals, concerts, and community

celebrations. Together, these elements create a rhythm of

activity that draws people through Uptown and supports

local businesses.

Trail connections, pedestrian corridors, and enhanced

streetscape design will tie these spaces together

physically and visually, creating a continuous “urban

walk” where each public space reinforces the next.

Thoughtful programming in central corridors,

including seasonal markets, outdoor movie nights, car

shows, holiday lighting events, community caravans,

parades, and other local and cultural celebrations will

further activate the spine and extend Uptown’s appeal

throughout the year—from Main Street, to the public

square, to Pastime Park, and beyond.

The public space network should be designed with

flexibility in mind, allowing spaces to function

independently for daily use or collectively for larger

community events. Design considerations such as

seating, shelter, lighting, and utilities should support ease

of use for both planned and spontaneous activities.

Implementation of this spine will require coordinated

planning among the Village, landowners, local

businesses, and community groups. Key priorities

include identifying opportunities to expand public

space where gaps exist, securing rights or easements for

connectivity, and leveraging event programming as a tool

for economic and social activation. Funding strategies

may include grant opportunities or local partnerships,

ensuring that construction and long-term maintenance

are fiscally sustainable.

The Uptown Public Space & Events Spine is more than

a collection of places; it is a community hub for social

interaction, cultural expression, and economic vitality.

The Depot at Public Square was officially completed in

May of 2025, providing a small event and meeting space

near the heart of the Village. Future phases of the Public

Square project include a park and a marketplace for

hosting year-round events.

6.5 Uptown Access & Parking Strategy

Balance parking supply and walkability to improve access

to Uptown and support businesses and residents.

Over the last few years, Uptown has attracted new

restaurants and destinations, drawing both residents

and visitors to spend more time in the area. As

additional development occurs, a thoughtful approach

to access and parking will be essential to maintaining

convenient access while enhancing walkability. This

strategy should evaluate the existing parking supply,

identify opportunities for shared or more efficient use,

and improve wayfinding to help visitors easily navigate

Uptown. At the same time, design improvements

such as safer crossings, key pedestrian connections,

and streetscape enhancements can enhance both the

appearance and functionality of Uptown. Providing

convenient and visible bicycle parking will also support

alternative transportation options and improve

overall access.

As demand evolves, the Village may consider longer

term strategies, including the potential for structured

parking in appropriate locations, while continuing

to prioritize efficient use of existing parking spaces.

Reducing or right-sizing parking requirements for

businesses in Uptown.

6.6 Catalytic Redevelopment Projects

Identify target project opportunities to attract investment

and revitalization in Uptown.

Strategically identify and promote key redevelopment

sites in Uptown to help guide investment and support

long-term revitalization. These efforts should focus on

underutilized or highly visible properties where targeted

improvements can create momentum and encourage

additional private investment.

Catalytic projects should prioritize a mix of uses that

bring consistent activity throughout the day, including

residential, retail, dining, and office or service-based

businesses. This mix can help generate steady foot traffic,

supporting a complete and engaging environment that is

active throughout the day and evening.

By defining clear opportunities aligned with community

goals and working with property owners and developers,

the Village can help shape projects that enhance

Uptown’s character and strengthen its role as a vibrant,

mixed-use destination. Supporting these efforts with

targeted marketing and communication tools will be

important to promote redevelopment opportunities,

attract investment, and clearly convey the Village’s vision

for key sites. Even small, well-placed projects can serve as

catalysts for broader reinvestment and continued growth.

PARTNERS

Ensure that Plain City’s

core services grow with

the community

Providing high-quality, reliable services is fundamental

to maintaining Plain City’s quality of life. As new

residents, jobs, and visitors continue to arrive, the Village

must ensure that essential services, such as public safety,

infrastructure, utilities, and community programs keep

pace. Plain City’s strategy is to scale its services while

strengthening partnerships with key providers, including

schools, safety agencies, utilities, and local organizations,

so that growth is supported in a coordinated and

sustainable way.

Today, many of the services that residents rely on are

delivered through a combination of Village operations

and trusted regional and local partners. Organizations

such as Jonathan Alder Local Schools, police and fire/

EMS providers, public works, the library system, Mid

Ohio Water & Sewer District, and community-based

nonprofits all play a vital role in supporting daily life.

As growth accelerates, demand on these systems will

increase, requiring not only additional capacity, but also

stronger coordination and long-term planning across

agencies.

Ensuring that services keep pace with development is

critical to avoiding gaps in response times, infrastructure

performance, and community support systems. This

includes planning for expanded staffing and facilities,

maintaining and upgrading infrastructure, and aligning

service delivery with where and how growth occurs. It

also means continuing to invest in relationships with

partner organizations, recognizing that many services

extend beyond Village boundaries and rely on shared

resources and collaboration.

By proactively scaling services and strengthening

partnerships, Plain City can mature in a way that is

stable, efficient, and responsive to community needs.

Coordinated service delivery supports public safety,

enhances daily life, and builds confidence in the Village’s

ability to manage change. The recommendations in this

section provide a framework for aligning growth with

service capacity, ensuring that Plain City remains a well

served, connected, and resilient community.

Public safety services are performing at a high level.

Emergency response times average approximately

five minutes, which is faster than many comparable

communities. This indicates strong service delivery under

current conditions.

Infrastructure systems are approaching or operating

at capacity, reflecting pressures associated with recent

growth. Th The Village’s wastewater treatment plant, which

has exceeded its current capacity of 0.75 million gallons

per day (MGD), is in the process of increasing capacity

to 1.25 MGD to accommodate increasing demand.

School enrollment has increased significantly over

time. J Jonathan Alder Local Schools serve over 2,400

students, a 31% increase since 2000. This growth reflects

broader population increases within the Village and

surrounding area.

Service provision relies on coordination across

multiple entities. Utilities, schools, and emergency

services involve partnerships between the Village and

external agencies. This structure shapes how services are

delivered across the community.

Changes to water and sewer infrastructure are critical

to supporting continued growth in the region.

Established in 2023, the Mid-Ohio Water and Sewer

District is advancing system expansion, including new

sewer lines along SR 161 and US 42, enabling phased

development and shaping long-term growth across Plain

City and Madison County.

Community services are closely tied to regional

systems and providers. Many services extend beyond

Village boundaries, requiring coordination at the county

and district level. This interconnected structure influences

service delivery.

approach, the Village can deliver reliable services, support community well-being, and

maintain a high quality of life as growth continues.

7.2 Public Safety & Civic Services Capacity Plan

Evaluate current service capacity and identify strategies

to ensure that service quality keeps pace with anticipated

growth.

In coordination with regional service providers,

Plain City should prepare a Public Safety and Civic

Services Capacity Plan that evaluates existing levels

of service and identifies where future growth may

place strain on systems. This assessment can be used

to inform actionable steps to expand capacity, improve

coordination, and maintain efficient, high-quality service

delivery across all departments. A proactive Capacity

Plan helps Plain City anticipate needs before they

become constraints, allowing the Village to continue

delivering quality services with excellent response times

for all community members.

Plain City currently provides civic and public safety

services through its Public Works Department,

Utilities Department, and the Division of Police. Fire

and emergency services are provided by the Pleasant

Valley Fire District, and water and sewer services are

managed by the Mid-Ohio Water and Sewer District.

The Capacity Plan should include a comprehensive

audit of these systems, including staffing levels, service

areas, infrastructure conditions, and the ability to

accommodate future growth. Coordination with Mid

Ohio, particularly around planned system upgrades and

capacity improvements, will be critical to ensuring that

utility infrastructure keeps pace with development.

Building on this understanding, the Capacity Plan

should identify infrastructure investment, services

expansion, or operational changes are needed to meet

future. It should also expand upon potential partnerships

identified in the Community Services and Partners

Roundtable (Recommendation 7.1), outlining how

coordination will be formalized and where shared

services or support may be appropriate. The Plan should

also identify services currently administered by the

Village that may need to be enhanced, expanded, or

supplemented through partnerships to accommodate a

growing community.

Through the adoption of a Public Safety and

Civic Services Capacity Plan, the Village will be

better positioned to align staffing, equipment,

and infrastructure investments with future needs.

Recommended improvements may include personnel

The Touch-A-Truck Event gives residents the chance

to connect directly with Plain City’s public service

departments. Events like this strengthen relationships

between the community and essential service providers,

ensuring trust and collaboration as the Village continues

to grow.

increases, equipment upgrades, and infrastructure

expansion budgets should be identified and integrated

into a Capital Improvements Plan (CIP) to help

the Village allocate future funding for necessary

improvements. External funding sources, like grants or

alternative financing models, should also be identified

in the Capacity Plan to help achieve future capacity

expansion goals.

7.3 Shared Outcomes, Different Roles: Jonathan

Alder Partnership Mode

Strengthen coordination with the Jonathan Alder

School District by clarifying shared goals, distinct

responsibilities, and collaborative approaches to

managing growth and community investment.

Jonathan Alder Local Schools currently serves a

104-square mile district and educates approximately

2,400 students within its five schools. As the region

grows with new residential development and expanded

utility capacity, it is imperative for Plain City and the

School District to plan for community changes to ensure

consistent, quality education is available for new and

existing residents within the school district boundary.

While the Village and the School District have distinct

roles, they share a common interest in managing change

in a way that supports long-term community stability

and quality of life. Establishing regular coordination

meetings will allow both entities to better understand

development trends, including the location, timing, and

scale of new housing, as well as evolving community

demographics. This coordination can improve enrollment

forecasting, help the District plan for future facility

needs, and ensure that services are aligned with

community growth.

The Village may choose to annex land and expand their

boundary, therefore it is also important to coordinate

how growth areas align with school district boundaries

and long-term planning for new or expanded school

facilities. Early communication can help avoid capacity

challenges and ensure that infrastructure and services are

in place to support new students and families.

The Village can also play a supportive role in advancing

School District initiatives, including future bond efforts

and facility investments. Strong schools are closely

tied to community success, influencing quality of life,

property values, and the Village’s ability to attract and

retain residents. Clear, consistent communication about

the importance of these investments can help build

broader community understanding and support.

170 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

The youth are the future of Plain City, and strong schools

and community programs, like Boy Scouts help build the

skills, values, and foundations they need to shape

that future.

In addition, coordination around transportation and

connectivity is critical. Ensuring safe and convenient

access to schools should be a shared priority, particularly

as new neighborhoods develop. This includes planning

for sidewalks, trails, crossings, and traffic management

strategies that support safe routes to school and reduce

congestion during pick-up and drop-off times.

By strengthening coordination, clarifying roles, and

aligning long-term planning efforts, Plain City and

Jonathan Alder Local Schools can work together to

ensure that growth supports both a strong school

system and a high quality of life. This partnership model

provides a framework for ongoing collaboration that

helps both entities respond proactively to change while

serving the broader community.

7.4 Coordinate with Utility Partners for Land Use &

Capital Planning

Create tools to assess current service offerings and

identify expansion plans that align with anticipated

development.

These tools provide clarity for developers, staff, and

service providers by illustrating where and how growth

can be supported. A service coverage map should identify

existing and planned water and sewer service areas,

including capacity and the timing of system expansions

in coordination with Mid-Ohio Water & Sewer District.

This allows users to understand where infrastructure is in

place and where upgrades or extensions may be required.

Together, these resources help define infrastructure

readiness by distinguishing areas that are development

ready from those that require additional investment.

They also support a more predictable and efficient

development review process, shifting from reactive,

case-by-case decisions to a clearer, more coordinated

approach.

The Service Coverage Map and Growth Review

Checklist also support implementation of the

Public Safety & Civic Services Capacity Plan

(Recommendation 7.2) by helping identify service gaps,

prioritize infrastructure improvements, and align growth

with available capacity (see maps of sewer coverage and

Mid-Ohio Water and Sewer District projects on page

70-72 of the Conditions and Trends Report in Appendix

A).

These tools should be maintained as a living resource,

updated regularly to reflect changes in service capacity,

infrastructure investment, and development processes.

Maintain a capable,

collaborative, and trusted

local government

Strong governance is the foundation for managing

growth, delivering services, and shaping Plain City’s

future on the Village’s own terms. As Plain City

plans for the future, proactive, transparent, and

regionally minded leadership is essential to ensure

that decisions, both inside and outside the Village,

reflect local priorities, protect community character,

and maximize opportunities. Plain City’s strategy is

to build internal capacity, strengthen staff expertise,

and foster collaborative partnerships with neighboring

municipalities, townships, and regional organizations, so

the Village can negotiate from a position of strength and

effectively implement the vision outlined in this Plan

Update.

Governance in a growing community requires

more than routine operations. It depends on clear

communication with residents, timely and accessible

decision-making, and the ability to engage in complex

regional conversations that affect land use, infrastructure,

environmental protection, and economic development.

Without sufficient capacity or consistent participation in

these forums, decisions may be made about Plain City

rather than with it, potentially undermining community

goals or leading to missed opportunities for strategic

growth, service delivery, or infrastructure investment.

Building strong governance also means investing in

staff, processes, and tools that enable the Village to

anticipate challenges, respond efficiently, and maintain

public trust. Transparency, accountability, and consistent

172 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Proactive communication and open collaboration with

regional partners can strengthen Plain City’s ability

to influence regional decision-making and strengthen

relationships with surrounding jurisdictions.

engagement create confidence among residents, partners,

and stakeholders, ensuring that the Village is seen as a

capable and reliable partner in regional planning and

development efforts.

By strengthening internal capacity and formalizing

regional partnerships, Plain City can manage growth

intentionally, protect its character and resources, and

deliver on the promises of this Plan Update. The

recommendations in this section provide guidance for

developing a clear fiscal and growth narrative, aligning

governance structures and staffing, and tracking progress.

Together, these actions position Plain City to lead

effectively, make informed decisions, and maintain public

trust as the Village grows.

Conditions & Trends

11

Departments and

Divisions

Plain City operates within a dual-county jurisdictional

structure. Th The Village spans both Madison and Union

Counties, resulting in differences in taxation, service

provision, and regulatory frameworks. This structure adds

complexity to coordination and decision-making across

jurisdictions.

The Village is experiencing steady population growth.

Plain City’s population grew by 51% between 2000 to

2023, from nearly 2,900 to over 4,300 residents, though

92% of that growth occurred between 2000 and 2010.

Administrative responsibilities are increasing in

complexity. Managing growth, infrastructure, and

services requires coordination across departments and

jurisdictions. This reflects the evolving operational

environment of the Village.

"We need to inform

residents what's in

the pipeline so we

can provide input

be-fore another

development is

approved."

Municipal operations are broad relative to the Village’s

size.  Plain City manages 11 departments and divisions

responsible for administration, infrastructure, and service

delivery. This reflects a wide operational scope that

requires coordination across multiple functions.

Plain City’s Mayor-Council-Administrator

governance model distributes authority between

elected leaders and an appointed administrator. Plain

City elected to adopt a Village Charter in 2021 that

establishes home rule charter, formalizing a governance

structure for city status. Residents elect the Mayor and

six Village Council members for four-year terms, while

Village Council appoints the Village Administrator.

8.2 “Plain City Growth Story” Transparency & Fiscal

Narrative

Develop a concise, accessible narrative that explains how

growth affects Village finances, services, operations, and

long-term sustainability to support informed public

decision-making.

As Plain City development pressure continues to grow

in and around the Village, clearly communicating how

that growth impacts the Village’s revenue, infrastructure,

and services will be essential to building trust and

supporting informed decision-making. The “Plain City

Growth Story” is intended to translate complex fiscal

and planning concepts into a clear, accessible narrative

that helps residents, stakeholders, and decision-makers

understand the relationship between growth, costs, and

long-term community outcomes.

This effort should articulate how different types of

development contribute to or impact Village revenues

and expenditures, including considerations such as

infrastructure costs, service delivery demands, and long

term maintenance obligations. By illustrating how land

use decisions influence fiscal sustainability, the Village

can better communicate trade-offs and priorities, helping

the community understand why certain development

patterns are better suited while others may present

challenges.

Tools such as visual dashboards, infographics, and

scenario-based comparisons can make this information

more relatable and transparent. For example, the Village

may consider developing a fiscal impact model or

land use return analysis to demonstrate how different

development types perform over time. These tools can

also support policy decisions related to annexation,

zoning, and capital investment by grounding discussions

in data.

The narrative should be updated regularly and integrated

into public-facing materials, Council discussions, and

future community engagement efforts. It should also

align with broader planning initiatives, including the

Comprehensive Plan, capital improvement planning, and

future plans or studies.

At its core, the “Plain City Growth Story” is about

building a shared understanding of how the Village takes

shape, providing the clarity needed to support thoughtful

growth, sustainable investment, and long-term success.

Theatre District Mural, Source: Lanesboro Arts

STORYTELLING CAMPAIGN

Lanesboro, MN

Once a declining rural town, Lanesboro

successfully reinvented itself by transforming an

abandoned rail line into the Root River State

Trail, investing in arts and culture, and preserving

its historic downtown to create a strong, authentic

identity centered on outdoor recreation and small

town charm.

This strategic pivot sparked a surge in tourism:

in 2024, the city recorded 1.1 million visitors,

supporting dozens of small businesses and

establishing Lanesboro as a regional destination.

The city was revitalized efficiently by leveraging

existing assets, strengthening walkable districts,

and building an experience-driven identity. These

investments align with Plain City's goals to

enhance corridors, elevate character, and create

vibrant, community-forward places.

COMMUNITY SURVEY

City of New Albany

A survey has been conducted each year since 2018

to measure community satisfaction within the

community. A consultant is hired to conduct the

survey. Surveys are administered via telephone and

online questionnaires. A sample of 300 adults was

surveyed. The survey is administered to residents

in which email or contact information is available;

they do not necessarily need to be registered to

vote. Results are published publicly.

8.3 Regional Growth & Conservation Roundtable

Coordinate planning with neighborhood communities

and regional partners.

Create a regular forum for coordination on growth,

conservation, and infrastructure priorities with adjacent

municipalities, townships, and regional partners. The

roundtable can foster joint planning, information

sharing, and collaborative problem-solving by identifying

shared challenges and opportunities, such as trails, key

corridors, the Big Darby Creek Accord, Mid-Ohio

Water Sewer District coordination, and alignment on

zoning, policy, and infrastructure. Formal agreements

or shared work plans can help strengthen partnerships,

reduce duplication of efforts, and support a cohesive

regional approach to managing growth while protecting

each community's character.

8.4 A Annual Plan Report Card & KPI Dashboard

Track progress on Comprehensive Plan goals using

measurable key performance indicators.

Develop a transparent, regularly updated dashboard

that tracks key indicators from the Comprehensive

Plan, including key metrics around land use, housing,

park and trails, transportation, and economic

development. Complement this with an annual report

card summarizing progress, highlighting success stories,

and identifying areas for improvement. Sharing this

information with Council, staff, and the public promotes

accountability, reinforces community priorities, and

supports data-driven decision-making.

8.5 A Annual Community Satisfaction Survey

Collect resident feedback to guide future priorities and

investments.

Conduct an annual survey to measure resident

satisfaction with Village services, amenities, and overall

quality of life. This feedback should be used to inform

future policy decisions, resource allocation, and planning

priorities. Surveys can be distributed through multiple

channels (e.g., online, mail, and in-person events) to

ensure broad participation. Include metrics that track

changes over time, and use results to identify emerging

concerns, gaps in services, or opportunities to improve

the resident experience. Consider partnering with

research groups, Jonathan Alder School District, local

businesses, and statically valid survey consultant to

ensure outreach is inclusive and representative.

IMPLEMENTATION

This chapter identifies priorities, partnerships, and

tools that will help Plain City translate the plan’s

goals into coordinated decisions and investments

The Comprehensive Plan provides a vision and set of recommendations

for Plain City’s future, but its success ultimately depends on consistent

implementation and ongoing collaboration. This chapter focuses on how

the Village can move from planning to action by identifying next steps,

establishing priorities, and outlining the roles that staff, elected officials,

community partners, and residents will play in carrying the plan forward.

Implementation is an ongoing process that requires coordination across

departments, boards and commissions, regional partners, and the broader

community. As decisions are made about development, infrastructure

improvements, parks, housing, and public services, the plan should serve as a

guide for evaluating opportunities and aligning projects with the community’s

shared goals. By working together and maintaining a long-term perspective,

the Village can ensure that individual decisions contribute to broader

community outcomes.

The Comprehensive Plan will also help inform future planning efforts and

investment decisions. It can guide updates to local policies and regulations,

support capital improvement planning, and help prioritize projects that

strengthen infrastructure, enhance public spaces, and support economic

development. Using the plan as a reference point for budgeting, programming,

and long-term investments will help ensure that growth is managed

thoughtfully and resources are directed toward the community’s most

important priorities.

Implementation should also be viewed as an adaptive process. As conditions

change and new opportunities emerge, the Village should periodically revisit

the plan, track progress on key initiatives, and make adjustments when needed.

Regular follow-up, coordination among partners, and continued community

engagement will help keep the plan relevant and ensure that Plain City

continues to move toward its vision for a resilient, vibrant, and connected

future.

IMPLEMENTATION 179

INTRODUCTION

The Plain City Comprehensive Plan was created for

the community and is intended to be used by Village

staff, Council, boards and commissions, and community

partners as they make decisions about the future of the

Village. The updated plan represents an important step

forward, positioning Plain City to manage growth while

addressing both emerging challenges and long-standing

community priorities.

This plan provides a framework to guide Plain City’s

evolution into a connected and inclusive community

that meets the needs of all residents through thoughtful

urban design, strategic investment, and people

centered solutions in housing, economic development,

and transportation. By establishing clear goals and

recommendations, the plan helps ensure that decisions

about development, infrastructure, and community

investment support the Village’s long-term vision.

This chapter focuses on how the Comprehensive Plan

can be used as an ongoing guide for decision-making. It

outlines how the Village can incorporate the plan into

daily operations, coordinate future planning initiatives,

and track progress toward key community goals. It also

highlights the importance of aligning the plan with

capital improvement planning, policy updates, and long

term investment decisions.

Through regular monitoring, collaboration across

departments and partners, and periodic updates,

the Comprehensive Plan can remain a practical and

adaptable tool. By using the plan as a shared reference

point, Plain City can continue advancing its goals while

protecting the character, values, and sense of place that

define the community.

Using the Plan

This Comprehensive Plan Update should be referenced

as part of daily operations as public and private decisions

are made concerning land use, development, capital

improvements, economic development, and other key

topics. Village staff familiar with the Comprehensive

Plan can introduce other department staff, boards,

and commissions to the document and train them on

how best to utilize it. Future Village planning efforts,

including capital improvement planning, strategic

planning, and focus area planning, should align with the

Plan. Additionally, the Village and staff should continue

to collaborate and hold planning workshops with their

departments and stakeholders to ensure that efforts

continue to align with their goals.

Monitoring the Plan

Regular review and monitoring of the plan

recommendations will help track implementation and

produce tangible outcomes. Plan review should happen

at a designated time no less than once per year. The

plan review should result in an annual progress report,

which can be shared with the community and Council.

The metrics provided within the implementation tables

for each topic area can be used as key indicators for

monitoring plan progress and success.

Update the Plan

This Comprehensive Plan Update is intended to be

durable and thorough, while still providing flexibility for

the Village and community partners to implement the

Comprehensive Plan to the best of their ability. Despite

this, a plan is a snapshot in time, and as change occurs,

the Plan will need to adapt to keep the community’s

vision in mind.

Village Council members Jamey Sintz and Tyler Harriman

Community Caravan open house, demonstrating how

Village staff’s active involvement and collaboration help

ensure the Comprehensive Plan is practical, achievable,

and responsive to community needs.

PUTTING THE PLAN TO WORK

Minor Plan Amendments

In the interest of preserving the agility of this planning document

so that it becomes a living expression of the Village’s endeavors, it

is recognized that minor amendments to the Plan may periodically

occur. These types of amendments should be limited to edits to plan

actions in the summary matrix or changes to the Future Land Use

Map. These amendments may be made in response to new project

or program ideas, changing circumstances or trends, or a unique

economic or development opportunity. Amendments to the summary

matrix shall require, at minimum, adoption of a resolution by Council.

In contrast, amendments to the Future Land Use Map should first

require a recommendation from the Planning & Zoning Commission

prior to Council considering adoption of a resolution. Annotations

to denote progress on particular action items in the matrix, or

establishing metrics to evaluate progress, shall not require formal

Council action.

Supplementary Plans

In the period prior to the next overhaul of the Comprehensive Plan,

more specialized planning efforts may occur and be adopted as formal

components, or supplements, to the current plan. These efforts should

involve at least some level of public engagement, in accordance with

the scale and significance of each effort. A more robust engagement

strategy should be employed for larger planning projects, such as an

update to the Village’s Thoroughfare Plan. Other efforts may include

focus areas or corridor plans. Supplementary plans shall require, at

minimum, adoption by resolution of Council.

Major Plan Updates

Typically, major updates to a comprehensive plan should follow a 10-

to-15 year cycle in response to demographic shifts, emerging trends

and technologies, and changes affecting physical growth patterns.

A major plan reboot should be a robust effort and engage many

stakeholders and community members. Such an overhaul should

also re-evaluate the community’s vision for the future and include an

analysis of significant issues and trends that Plain City is facing at

that moment in time. Major updates will require a recommendation

from the Planning and Zoning Commission, followed by adoption of

a resolution by Council.

IMPLEMENTATION 183

How to Use

The implementation matrix is a compilation of the

plan goals, strategies, and actions set forth in previous

chapters. This matrix connects each action with its

overarching goal and other essential information to help

organize and prioritize tasks.

Roles

Effective plans inspire real-world action, driven by

the community and guided by Village leadership.

Developing a plan and implementation strategy is only

the first step in a long-term journey toward meaningful

and lasting change. Many actions require coordination

with multiple stakeholders and partners, particularly

when financial, legal, or other constraints limit the

Village’s ability to act independently. Providing a clear

vision and actionable steps not only guides progress but

also empowers stakeholders to take ownership of the

process. By fostering collaboration and understanding

whether Plain City has control or influence over each

action, the plan becomes a unifying tool for collective

action and sustained impact.

• Control: Actions the Village can directly carry out

or integrate into its affairs.

• Coalition: Decisions and resources are shared with

2–4 peers. Convene, co-design, and memorialize

with a memorandum of understanding (MOU).

• Influence: Actions the Village seeks to advance but

cannot accomplish alone due to reliance on outside

partners or private decision-making.

• Advocacy:  Others must decide; the Village’s

role is persistent elevation and alignment (briefs,

champions, program recognition).

184 PLAIN CITY COMPREHENSIVE PLAN

Early Input from Advisory Committee Meeting 2 helped

inform the recommendations and priorities in the

implementation matrix.

Timeframe

The following distinctions will be assigned to each action

to identify whether they are more concrete and time

bound actions or general, ongoing policy directives.

• Ongoing:  General policy directives with no set end

date.

• Immediate (Next 6 months):  Actions or projects

that can be initiated and completed quickly.

• Short-term (6 months to 3 years):  Actions expected

to be completed within three years

• Mid-term (3 to 7 years): Actions requiring several

years, with progress by year seven.

• Long-term (7 to 10 years): Strategic initiatives

or projects needing extended planning and

implementation

IMPLEMENTATION 191

SUMMARY + NEXT STEPS

The Implementation chapter provides the essential link

between Plain City’s vision and real-world outcomes. It

serves as a practical guide for staff, councils, commissions,

and community partners, offering clear steps to apply,

monitor, and refine the plan’s recommendations over

time. This plan emphasizes collaboration and shared

responsibility, recognizing that achieving many actions

requires coordination beyond the Village’s direct control.

Built on extensive conversations with staff, stakeholders,

and residents, this chapter reflects the community’s

priorities and insights, ensuring that implementation

strategies are responsive, actionable, and grounded in

local context. By using the guidance in this chapter, Plain

City can take ownership of initiatives, track progress

through defined metrics, and adapt as circumstances

change, ensuring the Comprehensive Plan remains a

living document that continues to advance the Village’s

long-term goals.

Working Together:

Bringing the plan’s ideas to life will require strong

partnerships and continued collaboration across

the community. While the Plain City Departments

and Divisions will play a central role in leading and

coordinating many initiatives, lasting success depends

on the involvement of key partners, public agencies,

neighboring communities, institutions, organizations,

and residents working together toward shared goals.

These partnerships help ensure that projects are both

visionary and achievable, aligning resources, expertise,

and community priorities.

A. Introduction

Conditions and Trends Report | 6

In the midst of growth and change, Plain City is preparing for the future, while protecting

Why Update the Comprehensive Plan?

Community Context

+ Plain City is located at the western edge of the Columbus Metropolitan Area, one of the fastest

growing regions in the Midwest. The village is experiencing significant residential and commercial

development as development pressure from the greater Columbus area moves westward. Despite

recent growth, Plain City retains a distinct small-town character rooted in its historic Uptown core,

strong agricultural heritage, and scenic rural landscapes.

+ Regional connectivity is key. U.S. Route 42, US-33, State Route 161, and I-270 provide direct

access to regional job centers, with most major employment hubs less than a 45-minute commute.

This accessibility positions Plain City as both a desirable residential community and an emerging

employment center.

+ The community is closely tied to its natural environment. Big Darby Creek, a nationally recognized

scenic river, flows through the village and provides both a unique recreational amenity and an

environmental stewardship responsibility. Protecting this natural resource while accommodating

growth is a key priority for the community.

+ Plain City is at a pivotal moment. Recent projects and plans underscore the village’s efforts to

balance growth with preservation of community character. Together, these efforts envision a future

where Plain City serves as both a thriving residential destination and a vibrant local economy that

reflects its heritage, identity, and regional role.

B. Key Findings

Conditions and Trends Report | 10

Key Findings

The following section highlights

the key findings from the

Conditions and Trends Assessment.

These findings illustrate how the

community has evolved over

time, its current conditions and

challenges, and the opportunities

that will shape Plain City’s growth

in the years ahead.

B. Key Findings

Quick Facts

Population, 2023

4,900

Estimated Population, 2025

+895

+56%

Land Area Growth Since 2022

$87,018

Jobs, 2022
