Commute Elsewhere For Work

Conditions and Trends Report | 11

Key Findings

From Rural Roots to Rapid Growth...

+ Once a quiet village in Central Ohio, Plain City

has undergone a profound transformation in

the past two decades. Since 2000, Plain City’s

population has nearly doubled, growing from just

over 2,000 residents in 2000 to nearly 5,000

today.

+ Plain City functions largely as a bedroom

communitywith 96% of residents commuting

,

elsewhere for work and only 8.3% of local jobs

filled by residents. This imbalance places added

demand on local services and amenities without

capturing the full benefit of employment growth.

+ Housing construction, which slowed following

the Great Recession, has since exploded.  895

new housing units were permitted in the past five

years, nearly all of them single-family homes.

B. Key Findings Conditions and Trends Report | 12

Key Findings

From Rural Roots to Rapid Growth...

+ Plain City’s housing market has experienced

significant appreciation. Home values have more

than doubled since 2000, rising over $200,000

in median value, outpacing nearby communities.

The rising cost reflects both growing demand and

limited supply of housing.

+ Great schools, employment proximity, and

available land all contribute to housing demand

in Plain City. Central Ohio’s development

community is increasingly looking west, where

projects can be delivered more quickly, at lower

cost.

+ Job creation has not kept pace with population

growth.  Jobs growth has stagnated since

2012, and nearby closures such as RANCO in

2004 has impacted growth in complementary

manufacturing jobs.

B. Key Findings Conditions and Trends Report | 13

Key Findings

A Community in Motion...

+ Plain City is well-positioned within a booming

metropolitan region. As Central Ohio expands

toward a projected population of 3.15 million

by 2050, Plain City’s role is shifting from rural

crossroads to a maturing small town. The village is

seeing sustained residential growth, infrastructure

upgrades, and increasing development pressure.

+ Infrastructure investments are reshaping the

development landscape.  Major upgrades to water

and sewer systems are already underway, led by

the Mid-Ohio Water and Sewer District, which

now maintains and operates Plain City’s water

and wastewater facilities. These improvements are

essential to meeting environmental standards and

supporting future capacity.

B. Key FindingsConditions and Trends Report | 14

Key Findings

A Community in Motion...

+ Uptown Plain City, the community’s historic core,

continues its revitalization efforts guided by the

2018 Comprehensive Plan and the 2023 Uptown

Master Plan. Key efforts include Main Street

streetscape enhancements, expanded public

spaces, and infill development. These projects aim

to preserve the village’s identity while adapting to

growth.

+ At the same time, Plain City is investing in the

quality of life.  Public services, park amenities,

and trail systems are being expanded. Community

pride remains strong, with residents valuing

traditions, civic events, and natural features like

Big Darby Creek. These investments and amenities

now face the challenge of keeping pace with the

expectations of a growing population.

B. Key FindingsConditions and Trends Report | 15

Key Findings

Looking Ahead...

+ Plain City is projected to exceed 5,000 residents

by 2030, slated to become a city under Ohio law.

This milestone will mark more than a change in

municipal status, it reflects the community’s long

term evolution and increasing regional influence.

+ The next phase of growth will bring both

opportunity and complexity. As housing costs rise

and population diversifies, the Village will need

to address issues of affordability, job growth, and

adequate delivery of services. Upgrading facilities

and staffing, expanding housing types, attracting

local employment, enhancing transportation

infrastructure, and multi-modal connections are all

key topics to contemplate.

B. Key FindingsConditions and Trends Report | 16

Public Engagement

Input from residents, business

owners, and stakeholders will

shape the plan’s direction—

ensuring it reflects what

matters most to the community

and leads to thoughtful and

supported outcomes. This

process includes two public

workshops, online surveys, and

focused stakeholder group

discussions.

B. Key Findings Conditions and Trends Report | 17

C. Regional Conditions

Conditions and Trends Report | 18

Regional

Conditions

Plain City is located in one of the

fastest-growing metropolitan areas

in the Midwest. Understanding

the broader economic and

demographic context of the

Central Ohio region is crucial to

planning for Plain City’s future.

C. Regional Conditions

Included in this Section:

Population & Job Growth – Rapid job gains in modern

industries are fueling sustained population growth across

Central Ohio, reshaping communities region-wide.

Emerging Employment Sectors – Central Ohio continues to

attract Fortune-500 companies, logistics, and data centers.

Regional Housing Shortage  – Central Ohio is not building

enough homes to keep pace with population and job growth.

Regional Infrastructure Improvements – Mid Ohio Water

Sewer District is planning substantial centralized utility

expansions in Madison County, and Marysville continues invest

in water/wastewater expansion near Plain City.

MOWSD Projects

Mid-Ohio Water and Sewer

District is planning to extend

sanitary sewer along State Route

161, with a force main also

planned along US-42.

In the interim, the SR-161 line will

connect to Plain City’s existing

treatment plant. Long-term, flows

will be redirected south through the

US-42 force main to a new regional

treatment facility currently under

construction near London.

MOWSD Projects

Mid-Ohio’s proposed 5-year

sewer service area enables

substantial growth along S.R.

161 and US-42.

It is anticipated that some of the

resulting development enabled

by centralized utilities may

request annexation into Plain City.

However, areas not contiguous

to Plain City’s boundaries may

develop within unincorporated

areas of Madison County. These

investments threaten the village’s

ability to control growth responsibly

and prevent incompatible uses.

D. Conditions Analysis

Conditions and Trends Report | 30

Conditions

Analysis

This section presents a focused

scan of current conditions and

emerging trends in Plain City,

organized across seven topic areas

essential to understanding how the

community has evolved and where

it’s headed. Each topic pairs concise

key findings with supporting data,

maps, and sources. Together, they

provide a shared baseline for

decisions ahead.

Conditions and Trends Report | 32

Demographics & Workforce

The village is on track to reach city status by 2030,

reflecting sustained residential and population growth.

+ Significant population growth between 2000-

2010.  Plain City’s population grew by 51% between

2000 to 2023, from nearly 2,900 to over 4,300

residents, though 92% of that growth occurred

between 2000 and 2010.

+ Looking ahead, new development is expected to

push the population above 5,000 before 2030.

The current 2025 estimate is approximately 4,900

residents.

+ Union County is growing much quicker than

Madison County.  Since 2000, Madison County

grew by 9.7% (3,900 residents), while Union

County grew by 70.2% (28,700 residents).

*Note: Population estimate from Ohio Department of

Development’s 2023 Population Estimates. Since 2021, residential

permits for 895 additional housing units have been approved.

Once these units are built and fully occupied, this could add an

additional 1,500-1,800 residents to Plain City.

Conditions and Trends Report | 33

point to an increase in the number of families with children

Rising enrollment in Jonathan Alder Local School District

moving to surrounding communities and Plain City.

+ Top rated schools are a priority for new residents.

Jonathan Alder has a 95% graduation rate and ranks

in the top 20% of Ohio districts.

+ Enrollment in Jonathan Alder Local School District

has risen significantly.  Enrollment rose by 31.5%

between 2000-2025, from 1,776 to 2,406 students,

largely due to area-wide growth.

+ Enrollment is projected to surpass 3,000 by the

2034-35 school year. Facilities planning forecasts

enrollment growth based on new housing projects

inside the school district.

+ Junior and senior high school additions are planned

to prepare for enrollment growth. The facilities

master plan also includes new sports facilities and

parking expansion.

Conditions and Trends Report | 34

Most of Plain City’s residents work elsewhere.

+ Of the 2,257 employed Plain City residents, 96%

commute outside the village for work.

+ New waves of housing have reinforced this

dynamic. While residential growth has surged, job

growth has not kept pace, and local wages remain

lower than what local residents earn in surrounding

communities.

+ While Columbus accounts for

the largest share of Plain City’s

workforce, the majority come 1,004

Conditions and Trends Report | 35

Jobs available in Plain City are not the types of jobs that

are held by residents.

+ Educational attainment among Plain City’s

residents is generally higher than that of its

workforce.  34.3% hold a bachelor’s degree

or higher, compared to just 16.6% of the local

workforce. This gap reflects how residential growth

has attracted highly educated residents who

often work elsewhere, while local employment

opportunities remain limited in high-skill, high

paying positions.

+ The education gap between residents and the

local workforce is widening. Since 2010, the share

of residents with a bachelor’s degree or higher has

increased by 36%. Over the same period, the share +9.1%

of the local workforce with a bachelor’s degree

Residents with college

or higher has nearly halved, dropping by 41%

degree, 2010-2023

relative to 2010 levels. Rising home prices may be

exacerbating this existing gap.

Conditions and Trends Report | 37

Housing & Neighborhoods

Housing costs in Plain City are among the highest

in Madison County.

+ In 2023, over half of homes in Plain City were

valued between $300,000 and $500,000. This

price range is similar to new housing starts in

Jerome Township and Dublin.

+ Plain City’s median home value exceeds

neighboring counties.  The median home value is

$19,000 higher than Union County and $126,000

higher than Madison County.

+ Plain City’s median rent is $1,044, more affordable

than Madison County but less affordable than

Union County.

+ Home values have increased by over $200,000

(161%) since 2000. Median rent has also grown

significantly, rising by $500 (97%) in the same

period.

Conditions and Trends Report | 39

Relative to share of total units, Plain City added

significantly more housing after 2020 than its neighbors.

+ The majority of the village’s housing stock

is now built or approved post-2020. Since

2021, 895 units have been built or approved

for construction, which widens the age gap

between old and new housing stock.

+ Most homes were built either before 1939 or

after 2020. The large inventory of early 20th

century homes in Plain City, especially when

compared to nearby cities, creates a distinct

housing character in the village’s central core

and contributes to housing affordability.

Conditions and Trends Report | 41

Most new residential housing is traditional single-

family.

+ Nearly all new housing permits  are for single

family homes. Of the 783 residential permits

issued from 2021 to 2025, 97.1% (761) were

for single-family units.

+ Multi-family development is limited and

concentrated.  Most permits were for The

Residence at Maren Reserve, a single-story

townhome complex with 21 buildings and 132

units completed, and 35 buildings and 226

units planned.

+ The lack of “missing middle” housing limits

housing choice.  Duplexes, townhomes,

and small apartment buildings are scarce,

excluding many residents priced out of the

single-family market. However, this issue is

found across the region.

D. Conditions Analysis

The village grew around 882 acres since

Growth is driven

primarily by new residential land uses,

located primarily along the southern and

Plain City and the surrounding area is a

prime market for residential development.

Availability of large land areas surrounding

the village, proximity to regional employment

Conditions and Trends Report | 43

Land Use, Development, & Conservation

Land Use, Development, & Conservation

Key Findings

+ Conservation principles are at the forefront of

new development.  Open space and stormwater

management are facilitated through the village’s

development review process and updated Ohio

EPA policies.

+ Planned Unit Development (PUD) Districts

are commonly used for newly annexed

developments. The Planned Unit Development

District is the village’s primary tool to facilitate

quality, master planned development.

+ As the village looks ahead, it continues

to explore tools to manage growth and

development effectively. Tools like are available

to support neighboring local governments,

fund infrastructure improvements, and provide

amenities for residents.

D. Conditions AnalysisConditions and Trends Report | 44

Almost two-thirds of Plain City’s land area is

made up of agriculture and residential uses.

+ Recent annexations have introduced new

residential uses and future commercial

uses. New residential uses are primarily

south within Madison County.

+ Dedicated open space and stormwater

retention are emerging land uses. New

development is accompanied by large

swaths of parcels dedicated to required

open space areas and stormwater retention

facilities.

+ Undeveloped areas come in all different

sizes and locations. Vacant/undeveloped

parcels are scattered throughout the

village’s urban core, near the edges - slated

for future residential development, or

within environmentally sensitive areas.

New Development

The village has access to a wide range

of tools to support new development,

ensuring that future projects

contribute to a cohesive and thriving

community.

The following table outlines several

key mechanisms available to advance

economic development and manage

annexation in growing communities.

Many of these tools are guided by

the Village’s Economic Development

Incentive Policy, which shapes how they

are applied in practice. Each tool differs

in its purpose, implementation, and

effect on municipal boundaries or service

delivery coordination.

D. Conditions Analysis

Tool

Planned Unit

Development Design

Standards

Economic

Development

Agreement (CEDA)

Financing)

Community

Authority)

JEDD (Joint

Economic

Development

District)

Purpose & Mechanism

Village develops a regulatory framework

guiding layout, aesthetics, infrastructure,

and land use to ensure cohesive, quality

development.

Intergovernmental agreement (village +

townships) to share infrastructure, services,

and tax revenues for mutual economic

development.

Village finances new infrastructure by capturing

future increases in property or utility tax

revenue generated by development.

Special-purpose district providing infrastructure

financing via bonds/community charges

imposed on property within NCA boundaries.

Administered by a publicly appointed board.

Village and township jointly create a

development district, sharing income/property

tax revenue and providing municipal services

without annexing the land.

Big Darby Creek

Watershed

The health of the Big Darby Creek

begins upstream.

Plain City lies within the heart of the

Big Darby Creek Watershed, one of

Ohio’s most ecologically significant

waterways. The village covers

portions of three key tributary sub

watersheds, each playing a vital role

in maintaining downstream water

quality:

+ Robinson Run

+ Sugar Run

+ Worthington Ditch

Groundwater

Vulnerability

Areas with higher groundwater

vulnerability are at greater risk of

surface pollutants infiltrating into

the groundwater more quickly

and with less natural filtration.

NPDES Permits

NPDES Permits regulate the discharge

of pollutants into surface waters. These

could include wastewater treatment

plants, industrial facilities, and new

construction that disturbs one or more

acres.

Conditions and Trends Report | 63

Creekview

Commerce Park

Creekview Commerce Park will be

a 515-acre development that offers

a variety of commercial, flex and

industrial uses.

+ The project aims to create new

jobs  that align with nearby

industry clusters while also

attracting sectors that are

underrepresented in the region.

+ The park will fill gaps in local job

market - potentially reducing

the high rate of residents who

currently work outside the

village - and increase income tax

revenue.

Conditions and Trends Report | 64

The project balances economic growth

with quality-of-life and environmental

considerations.

+ Described as a “next generation

employment district,” its design includes

a high-quality public realm, campus

feel, and connection to trail systems and

natural features.

+ The commerce park will contain 60 acres

of preserved open space  and trails,

aligning with Big Darby Creek protection

efforts and buffering residential areas

from heavier uses.

+ Heavier, flex-industrial uses are located

internally to limit impact on nearby

neighborhoods.

Mid-Ohio Projects

Mid-Ohio Water and Sewer

District is planning to extend

sanitary sewer along State Route

161, with a future force main also

planned along US-42.

In the interim, the SR-161 line will

connect to Plain City’s existing

treatment plant. Long-term, flows

will be redirected south through the

US-42 force main to a new regional

treatment facility near London.

Development

Implications Along 161

Plain City’s 2018 Comprehensive

Plan outlines corridor development

principles for S.R. 161.

Development planned along OH

161 in Darby and Jerome Townships

will require coordination among

neighboring jurisdictions to ensure

quality, thoughtful development. Many

of the recommendations in the 2018

Comprehensive Plan remain relevant

today, given the extent of sewer

infrastructure improvements planned

for the corridor in the near future.

D. Conditions Analysis

Applicable Recommendations from 2018 Plan

Preserve sufficient right-of-way for future roadway expansion.

Right-of-way widths should provide enough room for “complete street”

improvements, including trails, buffers, and multi-use paths.

Manage access.

Vehicular curb cuts for development should be limited. Access should be

provided from existing or new roadways that intersect with SR-161, and

connected with a parallel roadway or drive, preferably to the rear of de

velopment sites. Intersection frequency should also be limited to align

with best practice access management standards.

Preserve a gateway experience.

For those traveling to Plain City via SR-161, the experience of driving

through the countryside before entering the village is a special one that

should be preserved.

Conditions and Trends Report | 82

Several boards, commissions, and volunteer committees support local decision-making.

Plain City is split between two counties – Differing rules, funding priorities, and systems

between Union and Madison Counties may lead to inconsistent development patterns.

+ Zoning and development must account for

county-specific regulations, utility service

areas and providers, or roads, which could

lead to differing growth patterns across

the county line.

+ Residents in each county may experience

variations in property tax assessments

,

health mandates, or county programming.

+ Differences in county investment

priorities  (such as infrastructure or social

services) may contribute to perceived or

actual inequities within the community.

+ The availability of county-level data

,

resources, and technical assistance may

differ.

Engagement Summary | 3

Round 1 Summary

Fall 2025

recurring themes emerged from this round.

Preserve Agricultural Land

Strong desire to preserve farmland and maintain the

village’s semi-rural surroundings.

Expand Community Gathering & Recreation

Invest in park improvements and other public spaces,

community events, and recreation opportunities.

Improve Connectivity & Mobility

Expand sidewalk coverage to enable safe pedestrian and

bicycle travel. Connect Plain City to the greater regional

bikeways system.

Manage Housing Development

Opinions toward housing are mixed, but the majority are

against significant housing development, particularly multi

family housing.

Grow Local Services & Amenities

Interest in more essential services like a grocery store

and pharmacy while keeping the small-town feel.

Protect Natural Assets

Protect the biodiversity of the Big Darby Creek and

other natural resources from future development.

Balance Growth with Preservation

Focus on growth and development that enhances

livability without compromising Plain City’s small town

charm.

Build on the Uptown Momentum

Support for mixed-use infill that enhances local retail and

dining options while maintaining historic character.

The following takeaways highlight what residents value about Plain City and where they see

Engagement Round 1: Key Takeaways

Engagement Round 1: Key Takeaways

4. Support local retail and essential servicesResidents want more amenities and conveniences

.

such as a grocery store, pharmacy, and retail without turning the city into a commercial hub.

+ Key takeaway: Seek opportunities for neighborhood-scale commercial and mixed-use

nodes. Pursue local retail and dining that aligns with village character over large modern

developments.

5. Balance housing needs with community values.  Many residents oppose large multi-family

developments but some are open to limited development of single-family, mixed-use or

diverse housing types if they fit the village character and help maintain

affordability.

+ Key takeaway: Develop a housing strategy that expands options

through design-sensitive infill and prioritizes single-family over multi

family.

Engagement Summary | 14

Round 2 Summary

Winter 2026

from Round 1 were reinforced by respondents in Round 2.

Preserve Natural and Agricultural Resources

Let conservation inform land use decisions by prioritizing

environmental stewardship, preserving farmland, and

maintaining the village’s semi-rural surroundings.

Balanced Economic Development in Uptown

Attract more businesses and amenities, with an emphasis on

locally oriented businesses and more offerings in Uptown,

including grocery stores, restaurants, and small businesses

over industrial development.

Invest in Parks, Recreation, and Community Spaces

Invest in expanding parks and recreation amenities, including

trails, natural areas, recreation facilities, and community

spaces that cater to families and seniors.

Traffic, Infrastructure, and Uptown Functionality

Proactively plan transportation and infrastructure, managing

through-traffic, improving safety and speed limits, adding

parking in Uptown, and planning for future utility capacity.

Manage Growth Thoughtfully

Plan future growth with intentionality, establishing clear limits

and policies to prevent overdevelopment, avoid strain on

infrastructure, and maintain Plain City’s small-town identity.

Improve Connectivity & Walkability

Expand sidewalks, trails, and safe crossings along major

corridors and between neighborhoods, parks, Uptown, and

regional trails.

APPENDIX 310

MEMORANDUM

To: Members of the Village of Plain City Council

From: Haley Lupton, Village Administrator

Date: June 29, 2026

RE: Resolution 27-2026: Amending the Village of Plain City Employee Policies and

Procedures Handbook

Background:

In routine review and inspection of the Village of Plain City Employee Policies and Procedures

Handbook, staff has identified several necessary revisions and has formally recommended the

implementation of such revisions. The proposed revisions are identified in Exhibit A as attached

to Resolution 27-2026. A brief summary of the proposed revisions is as follows:

• Section 4.4.4 (titled “Police Department Fitness Standard Incentive Program”): Creation of

a new section that formalizes the implementation of a physical fitness incentive program

for certain employees of the Plain City Police Department.

• Section 5.9 (titled “Unpaid Leave (Non-FMLA)”)”): Creation of a new section that addresses

policies regarding unpaid personal leave.

Objective:

To approve and implement amendments to the Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook.

Recommendation:

Staff supports and recommends the passage of Resolution 27-2026 at the second reading on

August 10, 2026. The amendments to the Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook have been

reviewed by the Personnel and Finance, CIP Committee accordingly, with a formal

recommendation of approval received from the Committee. Staff is confident that the

amendment furthers the goals and objectives of Council, specifically regarding the goal of

“continual development of organizational professionalism”.

800 Village Boulevard | P.O. Box 167 | Plain City, OH 43064 | 614-873-3527 | plaincityohio.gov

RESOLUTION NO. 27-2026

A RESOLUTION AMENDING THE VILLAGE OF PLAIN CITY EMPLOYEE

POLICIES AND PROCEDURES HANDBOOK

WHEREAS, the Village of Plain City Council previously approved Resolution 06-2026 which

implemented revisions to the Village of Plain City Employee Policies and Procedures

Handbook (the “Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook”); and

WHEREAS, the Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook is applicable to employees of

the Village of Plain City in order to provide a systematic and organized approach to the

establishment, implementation, and administration of the personnel policies and practices

relevant to all affected employees of the Village; and

WHEREAS, periodic reviews of the Village’s personnel policies and practices take place to

ensure compliance with state and federal labor laws and regulations and circumstances

that arise through the course of Village operations; and

WHEREAS, pursuant to the directives of the Village Council, amendments to the Employee

Policies and Procedures Handbook are proposed, as shown in Exhibit A attached hereto and

incorporated herein; and

WHEREAS,the Village Council has determined it necessary to amend the Employee Policies

and Procedures Handbook, as shown in Exhibit A attached hereto and incorporated herein.

NOW THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED by the Council of the Village of Plain City, Ohio, a majority

of the members elected or appointed thereto concurring, as follows:

Section 1. The Village Council hereby approves and authorizes the amendment of the

Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook, as identified in Exhibit A and attached hereto

and incorporated herein, with the language being added is in red and underlined.

Section 2. The Village Administrator and Finance Director are hereby authorized and

directed to ensure the implementation of the amendments to the Employee Policies and

Procedures Handbook and obtain acknowledgement by employees of the Village of their

receipt of such amendments.

Section 3. It is found that all formal actions of this Council concerning and relating to the

passage of this Resolution were adopted in an open meeting of this Council and that all

deliberations of this Council that resulted in this formal action were in meetings open to the

public in compliance with all legal requirements, including Section 121.22 of the Ohio

Revised Code.

Section 4. This Resolution shall be effective from and after the earliest period provided by

law.

Acknowledgment of Receipt of Policy and Procedures Handbook

I acknowledge that I have had the opportunity to review the Village of Plain City Policies and

Procedures Handbook and agree to abide by and be bound by its terms. I understand that it

is my responsibility to familiarize myself with the contents of this Handbook and all future

updates. I further understand that a copy of the Handbook is available in each Department

Head’s office and the Village Administration Office for review at any time. I understand that

it is my responsibility to ask my supervisor for clarification of any policy contained in this

Handbook.

I understand that this Policy Handbook is not to be construed as any type of employment

guarantee or employment contract, either express or implied, nor does it create any rights

for my employment that are not established by law.

If I work at the Police Department, I understand that this Handbook applies to me to the

extent the Police Department Operations Manual does not conflict with or address matters

addressed in this Handbook. The Police Department Operations Manual supersedes this

Handbook on all matters conflicting with or not addressed in this Handbook.

The Village of Plain City reserves the right to interpret, modify, revoke, suspend, terminate,

or change any or all such polices outlined in this Handbook at any time. As changes occur,

the Village of Plain City will distribute updates and clarifications as needed to ensure that

the Handbook remains current and accurate, and consistent with various local, state and

federal laws.

Village of Plain City

Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook

Introduction, Applicability and Administration

The provisions of the Village of Plain City Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook are

applicable to all employees, except as specifically provided herein. This Handbook’s

purpose is to provide a systematic and organized approach to the establishment,

implementation, and administration of the personnel policies and practices relevant to all

affected employees. This Handbook is not a contract of employment or a guarantee of any

rights or benefits, but is merely intended to be used to assist and guide employees in the

day-to-day direction and performance of their duties. Any promises or statements made by

any individual that conflicts with this Handbook is unauthorized, expressly disallowed, and

should not be relied upon.

The policies adopted in this Handbook supersede all previous written and unwritten

personnel policies or operational guidelines that directly conflict with this Handbook. All

previous written personnel policies or operational guidelines that do not conflict with this

Handbook shall continue in full force and effect. This Handbook is also intended to be

construed in such a manner as to comply with all applicable federal, state, and civil service

laws and regulations. Employees are responsible, as a condition of their employment, to

familiarize themselves with, and abide by, these policies and procedures.

The Village will endeavor to give employees advance notice of any Handbook changes.

However, the Village may revise these policies with or without advance notice. Notice of

revisions shall be provided to all employees. Employees are encouraged to make

suggestions for improvements in personnel policies and practices to their supervisors.

If any article or section of this Handbook is held to be invalid by operation of law, the

remainder of this Handbook and amendments thereto shall remain in force and effect.

Should a conflict arise between the Ohio Revised Code (O.R.C.) or applicable federal law and

this Handbook, law shall prevail. Additionally, should a direct conflict exist between this

Handbook and a Collective Bargaining Agreement, the Collective Bargaining Agreement

shall prevail.

2

Village of Plain City

Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook

Revised ___________, 2026

I. GENERAL EMPLOYMENT POLICIES

1.1 Equal Employment Opportunity

The Village of Plain City is an equal opportunity employer and does not discriminate

on the basis of race, color, religion, sex, age, national origin, disability, military

status, genetic testing, or other unlawful bias, except where such criteria is

considered a bona fide occupational qualification. All employees and applicants for

employment shall be recruited, selected, promoted, transferred, demoted,

terminated, suspended, evaluated or otherwise dealt with in a fair and equitable

manner, based solely upon merit, fitness and other bona fide occupational

qualifications and without regard to the above listed categories. The Village intends

for all of its policies to comply with federal and state equal employment opportunity

principles and other related laws.

1.2 Americans with Disabilities Act

In accordance with the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) of 1990, as amended,

the Village of Plain City prohibits discrimination in hiring, promotions, transfers, or

any other benefit or privilege of employment, of any qualified individual with a

permanent disability. To be considered a qualified individual, the employee must

satisfy the requisite skills, experience, education and other job-related

requirements of the position he or she holds or desires and must be able to perform

the essential functions of his/her position, with or without a reasonable

accommodation.

The Village will provide reasonable accommodation to a qualified applicant or

employee with a disability unless the accommodation would pose an undue hardship

on or direct threat to the facility. Decisions as to whether an accommodation is

necessary and/or reasonable shall be made on a case-by-case basis. Requests for

accommodation should be initiated by an employee and should be made directly to

an employee’s immediate supervisor. The employer and employee will meet and

discuss whether an accommodation is appropriate and, if applicable, the type of

accommodation to be given.

1.3 Employment Status

All employees of the Village not working for the Police Department shall be

classified as full-time, part-time, or seasonal, defined as follows:

1. Full-time employee - - an employee who regularly works at least forty (40) hours

per week.

2. Part-time employee - an employee who regularly works at least twenty (20)

hours but no more than twenty-nine (29) hours per week.

3

Village of Plain City

Employee Policies and Procedures Handbook

Revised ___________, 2026

3. Seasonal employee - an employee who works only during regularly recurring

seasons or periods of the year performing some work or activity limited to

that season or period of the year.

The classification of Police Department employees is set forth by ordinance

(currently titled “Providing for the staffing, organization and chain of command of the

Plain City Police Department”).

Full-time employees shall be entitled to all benefits provided by the Village, including

health insurance, life insurance, sick leave, vacation, bereavement leave, civil leave,

military leave, holiday pay, as provided by Village Council and all statutory benefits as

explained in this Handbook. Part-time, interim, contract and seasonal employees

shall be entitled to statutory benefits only.

1.4 Temporary Assignments

For purposes of this Handbook, a temporary assignment is defined as the

assignment of an individual employee to a classification different from his/her own,

which has substantially different duties than the employee would normally perform.

A temporary assignment shall be effected by the Department Head to meet the

operational needs of the department due to illness, emergencies or special

circumstances, and shall not be effected for disciplinary purposes. Temporary

assignments shall be for thirty (30) days or less. No employee shall serve more than

three (3) temporary assignments in any one calendar year. An employee temporarily

assigned to a position with a lower rate of pay shall maintain their current rate of pay.

This Section does not apply to employees working within the Police Department.

1.5 Personnel Files

The Village of Plain City Director of Finance shall maintain official personnel files of

all Village employees.

Each employee maintains responsibility for notifying his/her supervisor and the

Village Director of Finance in writing of any changes in personal addresses, phone

numbers, citizenship, selective service classification, marriage status, name,

emergency contact, personal deductions, etc., in order to allow for updating of

personnel files. Failure to report a change of address or telephone number may

subject the employee to disciplinary action. The Village shall provide information to

the employee regarding retirement system changes in beneficiary, dependent

hospital care, and other benefits subject to an employee's marital status.

Any change of address or phone number must be submitted within five (5) working

days of the change.
